4
Practical algorithms and experimental results

In this section, we develop two practical algorithms that are components of our proposed logical semantic communication system, aimed at the targeted query scenario of Theorem
5
and the scenario where Alice does not know what Bob knows addressed in Theorem
6
. These algorithms are based on linear coding concepts over the Galois Field with two elements
\{0,1\}
, denoted
GF(2)
, and are shown to be optimal on specific settings. We then present experimental results comparing the performance of an ensemble of methods, including the ones developed here, and contrast them with the
\Lambda
bound as well as optimized methods that treat the logic expressions as strings in the classic information-theoretic sense.

4.1
Linear codes for targeted queries

Let
X_{1}^{n}\in\{0,1,2\}^{n}
denote a random vector that represents three sets:
\{i:X_{i}=0\}
,
\{i:X_{i}=1\}
and
\{i:X_{i}=2\}
. The problem is to transmit to a receiver a vector
\hat{X}_{1}^{n}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
which agrees with
X_{1}^{n}
on all the positions
\{i:X_{i}\in\{0,1\}\}
as efficiently as possible, where efficiency is quantified as expected number of bits transmitted. One way to see this is that we are trying to efficiently send a partition that splits the sets
\{i:X_{i}=0\}
,
\{i:X_{i}=1\}
. The connection to the problem of semantic logic communication for the targeted queries scenario (Theorem
5
) emerges from the observation that sending a partition that splits the sets
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
and
{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}^{c}
is the key step in that problem.

We now present a general algorithm for partition compression based on linear codes over
GF(2)
. This algorithm can be applied even when we do not have any statistical model of the underlying sets for which we are creating a partition. In the special case where the entries of
X_{1}^{n}
are drawn
i.i.d. from
\{0,1,2\}
using probability masses (
p_{0},p_{1},1-p_{0}-p_{1}
), we will show that this algorithm is asymptotically optimal if
p_{0}=p_{1}
. The attractiveness of linear codes stems from the fact that they are easier to implement in practice.

For ease of analysis, we assume that the encoder and decoder share a random matrix with
i.i.d. entries drawn from
GF(2)
uniformly at random, and with
n
columns and an infinite number of rows, which we shall refer to as
G
; nonetheless, the number of rows we will effectively be using is only slightly larger than
n(p_{0}+p_{1})
. Define

\displaystyle\Psi=\{i:X_{i}\in\{0,1\}\}.

(20)

Let
G_{r}
denote the matrix obtained by extracting the first
r
rows from
G
, and for any given vector
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and set of indices
\xi\subset\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
, let
x_{\xi}
denote the
|\xi|
-long vector obtained by extracting from
x
only the indices given by
\xi
. The algorithm starts by finding the smallest positive integer
J
such that the equation

\displaystyle[M\cdot G_{J}]_{\Psi}=[X]_{\Psi}

(21)

can be solved for some
M\in GF(2)^{1\times J}
.
Exploiting
\delta
Elias coding
[
26
]
, the sender then sends the integer
J
to the receiver using

\displaystyle{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(J))}

bits, followed by the
J
bits in the message
M
. The receiver then decodes
J
and computes
M\cdot G_{J}
to retrieve the partition.

We now analyze the expected performance of this algorithm:

\displaystyle n^{-1}E_{J}\left[{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(J))}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E_{J}\left[J+\log_{2}J+2\log_{2}(\log_{2}J)+3\right]

(22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}E_{\Psi}\left[E_{J}\left[J+\log_{2}J+2\log_{2}(\log_{2}J)+3|\Psi\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n^{-1}E_{\Psi}\left[E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]+\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]+2\log_{2}(\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right])+3\right].

We next upper bound
E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]
. A sufficient condition to be able to solve Equation (
21
) is that
[G_{J}]_{\Psi}
has full-row rank, that is, the dimension of the space spanned by the rows of
[G_{J}]_{\Psi}
is exactly
|\Psi|
.

Let
W_{i}
be the smallest integer such that the row subspace spanned by
[G_{W_{i}}]_{\Psi}
has dimension
i
. We can then write

\displaystyle W_{|\Psi|}=W_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{|\Psi|-1}W_{i+1}-W_{i}.

(23)

The probability that a vector drawn uniformly from
GF(2)^{|\Psi|}
is nonzero, and therefore spans a space of dimension 1, is
1-2^{-|\Psi|}
; hence,

\displaystyle E\left[W_{1}|\Psi\right]=\frac{1}{1-2^{-|\Psi|}}.

(24)

We now analyze the difference
E[W_{i+1}-W_{i}|\Psi]
. Note that, by definition,
[G_{W_{i}}]_{\Psi}
spans a subspace of dimension
i
, which must consist of exactly
2^{i}
elements. If one chooses, uniformly at random, an element from
GF(2)^{|\Psi|}
, the probability that it lies within the subspace spanned by
[G_{W_{i}}]_{\Psi}
is
2^{i-|\Psi|}
. As a consequence, the probability that the subspace spanned by the rows of
[G_{W_{i}}]_{\Psi}
with such a random vector having dimension
i+1
is
1-2^{i-|\Psi|}
, and thus

\displaystyle E[W_{i+1}-W_{i}|\Psi]=\frac{1}{1-2^{i-|\Psi|}}.

(25)

Observing that
J\leq W_{|\Psi|}
, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]\leq\sum_{i=0}^{|\Psi|-1}\frac{1}{1-2^{i-|\Psi|}}\leq|\Psi|+2.

Continuing from inequality (
22
), we obtain an upper bound on performance as

\displaystyle n^{-1}E_{\Psi}\left[|\Psi|+\log_{2}(|\Psi|+2)+2\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}(|\Psi|+2)\right)+5\right].

To understand how good this bound is, we assume that the entries of
X_{1}^{n}
are drawn
i.i.d. from
\{0,1,2\}
using probability masses (
p_{0},p_{1},1-p_{0}-p_{1}
) and therefore
E\left[|\Psi|\right]=n(p_{0}+p_{1})
, which results in the following upper bound on average performance:

\displaystyle p_{0}+p_{1}+\frac{\log_{2}\left(n(p_{0}+p_{1})+2\right)}{n}+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}\left(n(p_{0}+p_{1})+2\right)\right)}{n}+\frac{5}{n}.

(26)

Now assume that
p_{0}=p_{1}
. The achievable Shannon limit for this setting is given by

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{0},p_{1})=\Lambda(p_{0},p_{0})=2p_{0},

which is the same performance as in (
26
) asymptotically as
n
grows. If
p_{0}\neq p_{1}
, then
\Lambda(p_{0},p_{1})<p_{0}+p_{1}
and thus our linear code construction is not optimal.

4.2
Nonlinear codes for targeted queries

We next present an example of a small nonlinear code for the case
n=6
that is optimal. In this setting, we assume that
X_{1}^{n}\in\{0,1,2\}
with exactly one entry of
X_{1}^{n}
equal to 0, and exactly one entry equal to 1.

One way to send a partition that separates the (single entry) sets
\{i:X_{i}=0\}
and
\{i:X_{i}=1\}
is to send an integer in the set
\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
identifying the one element in, say, the first set. The cost of this is
\log_{2}6
bits. Alternately, one can scan each of the 4 codewords below to find one that matches with
X_{0}X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}X_{5}
on the 0s and 1s, regarding the 2s as “don’t care”:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\mbox{ sender vector ($X_{i}\in\{0,1,2\}$) }&X_{0}&X_{1}&X_{2}&X_{3}&X_{4}&X_{5}\\
\hline\cr\mbox{first codeword}&0&0&0&1&1&1\\
\mbox{second codeword}&0&1&1&0&1&0\\
\mbox{third codeword}&1&0&1&1&0&0\\
\mbox{fourth codeword}&1&1&0&0&0&1\end{array}.

We observe that any two columns of this binary matrix contain at least one row with the pattern “0 1” and one row with the pattern “1 0”. For example, if
X_{0}=1,X_{1}=2,X_{2}=2,X_{3}=2,X_{4}=0,X_{5}=2
the third and fourth codewords are valid codewords.

Therefore, one can always find one such codeword, which can be specified using
2<\log_{2}6
bits.

This small example can in fact be extended easily. Notice that the columns of the matrix are exactly the set of all binary patterns with 2 ones (in the parlance of coding theory, the columns have weight 2), and thus this poses the interesting question of what the properties are of matrices whose column weights are a constant. One such property is easy to deduce, as stated in the following result.

Lemma 3
(Constant column weight codes)

Let
c
be a
t\times n
binary matrix where every column has exactly the same weight
w
, and any two columns are different. Then the result code partitions any two sets each comprising exactly one (but different) integer in the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
.

Proof.
Let
i,j
be the indices of any two distinct columns of the matrix
c
. The problem is to demonstrate that there is a row
k
such that
[c_{k,i},c_{k,j}]=[0,1]
and that there is another row
k^{\prime}
where
[c_{k^{\prime},i},c_{k^{\prime},j}]=[1,0]
. Supposing that neither of these conditions is true, then we deduce that
[c_{k,i},c_{k,j}]\in\{[0,0],[1,1]\}
for all
0\leq k<t
and thus necessarily the two columns are identical, which contradicts the assumption of the lemma. Suppose that, say, the first condition is true, but not the second one. Then it must be the case that the second column indexed by
j
has a strictly larger weight than the column indexed by
i
, which is also a contradiction of the assumptions in the lemma. The case where the second condition is true but not the first one is dealt with similarly.
\hfill\square

It is possible to obtain, for any given desired length
n
, a crude bound on the minimum number of rows
t
in a
t\times n
binary matrix
c
that partitions two sets each comprising exactly one non-overlapping integer in
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Take any one row of the matrix
c
, and assume it has
n_{0}
zeros and
n_{1}
ones. The number of patterns with exactly one 0, one 1, and the rest don’t cares, that can be handled by any one row is at most
n_{0}n_{1}\leq n^{2}/4
. Therefore the entire matrix
t
can handle at most
tn^{2}/4
patterns. There are a total of
n(n-1)
patterns that we need to handle, and therefore the following relation must always hold:

\displaystyle t\geq 4\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right).

Rounding up (since
t
is an integer), we see that at least 4 rows are needed for any value of
n>1
, showing that our
4\times 6
code is optimal in this sense. This bound is obviously too loose for anything other than
n=6
.

4.3
Linear codes for the the case Alice does not know what Bob knows

Let
X_{1}^{n},Y_{1}^{n}\in GF(2)^{n}
represent two random vectors with the property that
\{i:X_{i}=1\}\subseteq\{i:Y_{i}=1\}
. We assume that Alice knows
X_{1}^{n}
but not
Y_{1}^{n}
(other than the condition above), and that Bob knows
Y^{n}_{1}
. The goal is to efficiently transmit
X_{1}^{n}
to Bob. The connection to the problem alluded to in the title of this subsection arises by identifying
X_{1}^{n},Y_{1}^{n}
with the kernels of
\mathtt{S}
and
\mathtt{R}
, respectively.

In what follows we show a practical method based on linear codes. Let
\Delta>0
be an integer that is a design parameter. For convenience, we assume that Alice and Bob share a matrix
G
with dimensions
(n+\Delta)\times n
and entries drawn uniformly and independently at random from
GF(2)
.

Let
\Psi=\{i:Y_{i}=1\}
. The method starts with Bob transmitting to Alice the integer
|\Psi|
. At this point both Alice and Bob will keep only the first
|\Psi|+\Delta
rows of
G
, denoted
G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}
. Alice sends to Bob the bits resulting from the multiplication
G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}X_{1}^{n}
, where
X_{1}^{n}
is interpreted as a column vector. Let
[G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}]_{\Psi}
denote the matrix obtained by extracting from
G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}
the columns implied by the indices
\Psi
; note that this matrix is computable by Bob but not Alice. Bob then attempts to solve the equation

\displaystyle[G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}]_{\Psi}z=G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}X_{1}^{n}

(33)

for a unique
z
. If such a unique
z
exists, Bob can retrieve
X_{1}^{n}
by making use of the fact that the only entries of
X_{1}^{n}
that could possibly be equal to 1 must have an index contained in
\Psi
and the fact that

\displaystyle[X_{1}^{n}]_{\Psi}=z,

where
[X_{1}^{n}]_{\Psi}
denotes the entries of
X_{1}^{n}
subset to the indices in
\Psi
. Therefore
X_{1}^{n}
can be recovered from
z
by lifting the latter using the indices
\Psi
.

By construction, (
33
) has at least one solution. If the
|\Psi|
columns of
[G_{|\Psi|+\Delta}]_{\Psi}
are linearly independent, then that solution must be unique. The probability that these columns are linearly independent is given by
\Pi_{i=0}^{|\Psi|-1}(1-2^{i-|\Psi|-\Delta})
and can be lower bounded in this manner:

\displaystyle\Pi_{i=0}^{|\Psi|-1}(1-2^{i-|\Psi|-\Delta})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{i=0}^{|\Psi|-1}\log(1-2^{i-|\Psi|-\Delta})\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{i=0}^{|\Psi|-1}-\frac{2^{i-|\Psi|-\Delta}}{1-2^{i-|\Psi|-\Delta}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{|\Psi|}-\frac{2^{-i-\Delta}}{1-2^{-i-\Delta}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{|\Psi|}-2^{-i-\Delta+1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-2^{-\Delta+1}\sum_{i=1}^{|\Psi|}2^{-i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-2^{-\Delta+1}\right).

Bob can signal to Alice success or failure in finding a unique
z
with a single bit, and in the case of failure, Alice can simply send
X_{1}^{n}
verbatim by sending
n
bits.

The normalized expected number of bits transmitted in either direction is then upper bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\left(1-\exp\left(-2^{-\Delta+1}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{n}\left(E\left[|\Psi|\right]+\Delta+E\left[{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(|\Psi|))}\right]+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{-\Delta+1}+O\left(2^{-2\Delta}\right)+\frac{1}{n}\left(E\left[|\Psi|\right]+\Delta+\log_{2}E\left[|\Psi|\right]+\log_{2}\log_{2}E\left[|\Psi|\right]+4\right).

Define
q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}n^{-1}E\left[|\Psi|\right]
. With this definition, then the upper bound may be summarized as

\displaystyle q_{1}+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}n}{n}\right)+2^{-\Delta+1}+O\left(2^{-2\Delta}\right),

(34)

where the
O(\cdot)
terms are to be interpreted with respect to the
n\rightarrow\infty
and
\Delta\rightarrow 0
limits, respectively.

To understand how good the above bound is, assume temporarily that
Y_{1}^{n}
has entries drawn i.i.d. from
\{0,1\}
using probability masses
\{1-q_{1},q_{1}\}
, and that each
X_{i}
is drawn using the conditional distribution

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1|Y_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{1}/q_{1},

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0|Y_{i}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

Under these assumptions for
X_{1}^{n},Y_{1}^{n}
, a lower bound is given by
n^{-1}H(X_{1}^{n}|Y_{1}^{n})=q_{1}H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{1}/q_{1}\right)
. Comparing this lower bound to (
34
), we see that if
q_{1}=2p_{1}
,
n
is sufficiently large and
\Delta
is sufficiently small, then the linear coding algorithm can arbitrarily approach the lower bound. For other choices of
q_{1},p_{1}
, nonetheless the algorithm is not optimal.

4.4
Experimental setup

The goal of this section is to illustrate the possible gains that one may expect from a practical semantic communication system compared to a classical one, and to also show the existing gap between such semantic communication system with respect to the ultimate bound given by
\Lambda
.

A first problem is the fact it is possible to portray classical communication systems to be nearly arbitrarily inefficient when compared to semantic ones. The reason is the multiple different ways in which sentences can express the same underlying semantic content; classic compression systems must be faithful to the original sentence itself whereas semantic systems as regarded in this article can take advantage of the intended meaning of the symbols within the sentence. To illustrate this problem, note that given
H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\mathtt{S})=0
,

\displaystyle H(\mathtt{S})=H(\mathtt{S}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})})+H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})\geq H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}).

(35)

The gap
H(\mathtt{S}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})})
can be very large; consider, for example, propositional logic in two variables
X_{1},X_{2}
and note that

(X_{1}\land X_{2})\lor\lnot X_{3}

\lnot(\lnot(X_{1}\land X_{2})\land X_{3})

\lnot((\lnot X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{2})\land X_{3})

\lnot((\lnot X_{1}\land X_{3})\lor(\lnot X_{2}\land X_{3}))

\lnot((\lnot X_{1}\land X_{3})\lor(\lnot X_{2}\land X_{3}))\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})

\lnot((\lnot X_{1}\land X_{3})\lor(\lnot X_{2}\land X_{3}))\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})\land(X_{1}\lor\lnot X_{1})

are all logically equivalent sentences. To be clear, one can legitimately expect semantic communication systems to take advantage of the observation (
35
), but one must exercise caution and not overstate this. Our paper’s theoretical results not only leverage the phenomenon (
35
), but go beyond and exploit more delicate findings on how targeted queries may admit unusually efficient representations or how, in some occasions, communication is surprisingly just as efficient when Alice doesn’t know Bob’s sentence
\mathtt{R}
as when she does know it.

Our experimental evaluation methodology follows the philosophy of always choosing a stronger “classical” (non-semantic) baseline to compare against whenever a choice is on the table, even if these baselines start to become more semantic in nature. In particular:

•

On purpose, we will forego the type of advantage that stems from observation (
35
) even though we can legitimately claim it.

•

Logic sentences will be optimized so that they can be represented more compactly by “classical” compressions systems.

4.4.1
Scenarios

We demonstrate two contrasting scenarios, derived from Theorem
7
and Theorem
6
, respectively:

•

(Targeted query with a shared statement)
After the communication, Bob will be able to prove
\mathtt{Q}
, which satisfies
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
. Both Alice and Bob know
\mathtt{R}
; only Alice knows
\mathtt{S}
; we simplify to the setting
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
.

•

(Alice doesn’t know what Bob knows)
After the communication, Bob will be able to prove all that Alice can. Only Bob knows
\mathtt{R}
, and only Alice knows
\mathtt{S}
. We assume
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
.

4.4.2
Test case generation

The reader shall recall that all of our theorems have an upper bound that holds in significant generality, and a corresponding lower bound that holds under additional assumptions. We have chosen distributions for
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
and
\mathtt{R}
that meet these additional assumptions to enable comparisons with both upper and lower bounds. In particular, their distribution is chosen so that:

•

\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
,
\mathtt{Q}\vdash\mathtt{R}
(and therefore
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
);

•

The random tuples
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))_{i}\}
are i.i.d.;

•

The Markov chain
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow({\kappa(\mathtt{S})},{\kappa(\mathtt{R})})
holds.

A simple way to construct
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
with these properties is to choose their underlying kernels first, and then construct sentences from those kernels. If the reader has, instead, a collection of sentences meeting the entailment constraints which are believed to come from an otherwise unknown distribution, then the reader may still obtain value from our paper by considering only the upper bounds.
We generated random kernels meeting all the three constraints above on sets of
10
variables to test our methods in practice. For each of the kernels we produced a generalized decision tree with the given kernel as its set of satisfying truth value assignments. We then post-processed each sentence by writing it using postfix notation, compressing variable names and removing spaces. For the targeted query scenario we have chosen
p_{r}=0.5
,
p_{s}=0.075
and
p_{q}
by sampling the range
[p_{s},p_{r})
uniformly. For the scenario where Alice does not know what Bob knows, we set
p_{s}=p_{q}=0.075
and let
p_{r}
range in
(p_{s},0.5]
.

We generated
1000
test cases for each choice of
(p_{s},p_{q},p_{r})
considered. This data could be used, through appropriate subselection, to illustrate all of the theorems in this article in fullness with the exception of Theorem
7
. In this theorem the assumption
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
is simply omitted. Therefore only those subcases where it is true can be demonstrated with this data.

4.4.3
Method of Generating Kernels

Since the sender’s kernel is a subset of the receiver’s kernel, and, moreover, the query is provable by the sender but not the receiver, it follows that the sender’s kernel is contained in the kernel associated with the query, which in turn is contained in the receiver’s kernel. It then follows that we can go through all the possible truth value assignments for the 10 variables and, for each assignment
\mu
, generate a random number
\eta\in[0,1]
where, if
\eta\leq p_{r}
, we place
\mu
in the receiver’s kernel. If additionally
\eta\leq p_{q}
, then we can place
\mu
in the query kernel, and if additionally to that
\eta\leq p_{s}
, then we can place
\mu
in the sender’s kernel. Pseudocode for this simple procedure is given in Algorithm
1
in Appendix
C.1
.

4.4.4
Method of Generating Generalized Decision Tree Sentences

The gist of the decision tree approach is to recursively call a method
\mathtt{GENERATE\_GDT\_FOR\_KERNEL(\cdot)}
to find a most balanced variable
\mathtt{X_{b}}
among the variables
\mathtt{X_{1},...,X_{n}}
– meaning a variable where
\mathtt{X_{b}}=1
and
\mathtt{X_{b}}=0
as close to equally as possible among the kernel elements – and then output

\displaystyle(\mathtt{X_{b}}\land\mathtt{GENERATE\_GDT\_FOR\_KERNEL(pos\_kernel}))\vee

\displaystyle(\neg\mathtt{X_{b}}\land\mathtt{GENERATE\_GDT\_FOR\_KERNEL(neg\_kernel})),

where
\mathtt{pos\_kernel}
is the reduced kernel on the
\mathtt{n-1}
variables
\mathtt{X_{1},...,X_{n}}
but with
\mathtt{X_{b}}
excluded, and in the original kernel
\mathtt{X_{b}}=1
, while
\mathtt{neg\_kernel}
is the reduced kernel on the
\mathtt{n-1}
variables
\mathtt{X_{1},...,X_{n}}
but with
\mathtt{X_{b}}
excluded, and in the original kernel
\mathtt{X_{b}}=0
. The generalized version of this heuristic is a bit more nuanced so that variables that are effectively constant with respect to the kernel (in other words, there is a variable
\mathtt{X_{i}}
such that either
\mathtt{X_{i}}=1
or
\mathtt{X_{i}}=0
for every kernel element) are efficiently split out, and when there are
\mathtt{k}
variables remaining and the kernel size is either
2^{\mathtt{k}}
or
0
the routine immediately terminates, outputting an empty string (equivalent to outputting True) in the former case, and outputting a False indicator in the latter case. The pseudocode for implementing the generalized decision tree algorithm, given a kernel, along with an accompanying detailed description and worked example are provided in Appendix
C.2
. There is a considerable literature on the use of decision trees to represent Boolean functions. See, for example,
[
14
,
52
,
56
]
.

4.5
Experimental results

4.5.1
Semantic communication systems

In a first set of experiments, we aim to demonstrate the performance of our practical semantic communication techniques against the new theoretical bounds. We have implemented the linear codes described in Subsections
4.1
and
4.3
which are used to implement the two scenarios in Subsection
4.4.1
. Due to the fact that these linear codes are not always optimal, we augmented these systems with “näive” semantic communication strategies based on efficient lossless transmission of kernels. In certain parameter ranges these are better than the linear codes so in those cases, we can use them instead. In the case of targeted queries, the task of allowing Bob to prove
\mathtt{Q}
can be alternately accomplished by sending to him
{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}
or
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, whichever is cheapest. The results, averaged across all 1000 test cases for each choice of parameters, can be found in Figure
11(a)
, where in blue, we illustrate the performance of linear codes, and in green and red we illustrate the performance of sending
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
and
{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}
using enumerative source codes
[
20
]
, respectively. In each of these three practical systems, we include a line of the same color that is a lower bound on performance for the specific technique. The bolder dots are those that are closest to the limiting Shannon bound, which is the bold, lowest plot shown in the figure. Similarly, in the case that Alice doesn’t know what Bob knows, the task of allowing Bob to prove
\mathtt{S}
can be alternately accomplished by sending to him
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, also using enumerative source coding
[
20
]
. The results of this experiment can be found in Figure
11(b)
. It can be appreciated that our practical codes can be quite efficient in some scenarios, but in general, more work is needed to develop practical codes that meet the Shannon bound in general.

((a))

Semantic communication systems for targeted queries with a shared sentence with
p_{r}=0.5
,
0.075<p_{q}\leq 0.5

((b))

Semantic communication systems for when Alice doesn’t know what Bob knows with
p_{s}=p_{q}=0.075

Figure 11
:
Comparison of practical semantic communication methods against the Shannon bound
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})
for two scenarios.

4.5.2
Classic communication systems

In a second set of experiments, we want to contrast practical classic systems with semantic ones. In these experiments, we will reuse the best results from practical methods in Subsection
4.5.1
and compare those with classic compression systems.

We define classic compressions systems as ones that may only use the sentence as presented to the communication system, and not perform operations on it with awareness of its underlying semantic content. These are examples of such systems:

•

(Targeted query with a shared statement)
Using the Decision Tree representation of a sentence, employ a standard compression algorithm (in particular, gzip, lzma, bzip2) to compress
\mathtt{S}
and
\mathtt{Q}
; choose the best representation and send that one.

•

(Alice doesn’t know what Bob knows)
The same as above, but considering only
\mathtt{S}
as in this scenario,
\mathtt{Q}=\mathtt{S}
.

We provide an additional advantage to the type of classic communication systems used above: we compress all 1000 samples simultaneously, which allow the compression algorithms described above to leverage patterns that only emerge when more data is available. In contrast, the semantic communication systems are compressing only one instance at a time, which is a much more difficult target. We reiterate that by using Decision Trees, we have already used semantic concepts to benefit the classical baseline. The results of these experiments can be found in Figures
12(a)
and
12(b)
. These plots normalize the performance of the practical semantic or classical systems against the corresponding Shannon bound
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})
and
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})
, respectively. We acknowledge that in the realm of classical communication systems we should consider Slepian-Wolf compression algorithms
[
68
]
as a baseline. This is particularly difficult to do as we are not aware of practical instances of such algorithms that would be applicable to the complex statistical distributions present in
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}
; we leave this comparison as an open item for future research.

((a))

Targeted queries with a shared sentence (
p_{r}=.5
,
p_{s}=.075
)

((b))

Alice does not know Bob’s sentence(
p_{s}=p_{q}=.075
)

Figure 12
:
Contrasting classic and semantic communication systems relative to the semantic Shannon limit

5
Formal information-theoretic results

The purpose of this section is to formally state and prove each of the information-theoretic results we offer in this paper.

5.1
Full ignorance

In this first problem, Alice is in possession of a logic sentence and wishes to communicate to Bob with the goal that whatever logic inferences Alice can make starting from that sentence, Bob, who otherwise knows nothing, can do the same. Crucially, Bob is not required to reproduce the particular way in which Alice’s logic sentence is represented in her mind, but rather it just needs to be able to retrieve a functionally equivalent sentence. The intention of this result is mainly to introduce notation and concepts as we build up to more interesting cases.

As with all our theorems, Theorem
2
has a lower bound on performance, as well as an algorithm to achieve that performance. We sketch next that algorithm, and then formally prove both upper and lower bounds. Alice and Bob meet in preparation for a future where
\mathtt{s}
is revealed to Alice. Given that any possible query that is entailed by
\mathtt{s}
must be proved by Bob (see Figure
13
, which illustrates the kernels of two possible queries
\mathtt{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{q}^{\prime}
with the property that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}^{\prime}
), and given that this must be done as efficiently as possible, they agree that the sensible goal is not to send her sentence
\mathtt{s}
verbatim, but to send the kernel
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
instead. From this, Bob can use the function
\ell
to recover a functionally equivalent sentence
\mathtt{\hat{s}}
.

To implement this, our suggested algorithm is for Alice to first send to Bob the
size
of the kernel. Once this size is transmitted, all possible kernels can be enumerated by both Alice and Bob and then Alice can simply send the index of the kernel that she has in her possession. To implement the idea above we need two tools: a means for efficiently sending integers (to encode the size) and a way to efficiently encode the kernel indices. The same needs will recur in all of our results, so we pause here to introduce two widely known tools to accomplish this.

For integer encoding, we use

\delta
Elias coding
[
26
]
. This code assigns codewords to each integer, where the length of a codeword for the integer
n\geq 1
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(n))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lfloor\log_{2}(n)\rfloor+2\lfloor\log_{2}(1+\lfloor\log_{2}(n)\rfloor)\rfloor+1\mbox{ bits}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\log_{2}(n)+2\log_{2}\log_{2}(n)+3\mbox{ bits}.

Our rationale for choosing this particular code is that it is both convenient and sufficient to prove asymptotically good results. For the problem of sending a kernel index, techniques for enumerating members of a set and efficiently sending and receiving indices from such enumeration can be found in the work of Cover on enumerative source coding
[
20
]
. This overall strategy is described at the bottom of Figure
13
.

We now formally present our main result for this case followed by its proof.

Theorem 2
(Bob can prove all Alice is able to)

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S}\in\mathcal{L}
have a kernel that follows a
p_{s}
-law. Let the encoder
f
and decoder
g
be functions

\displaystyle f:\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},

\displaystyle g:\{0,1\}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L},

respectively. Then,

\displaystyle\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right]\leq|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\leq H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right)+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right),

where the minimization is over
f,g
such that the image of
f
is prefix-free and such that, if
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, then
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s}))
and
g(f(\mathtt{s}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
. Under the same assumptions for
f,g
, the following lower bound holds:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})\leq\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right].

(36)

Furthermore, if we additionally have that the
\{\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}\}_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d., then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{s})=H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right)\leq\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right].

{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q^{\prime}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q^{\prime\prime}})}

(a)
\mathtt{s}
\kappa
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
ennumerative
code
decoder
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
\ell
\mathtt{\hat{s}}
{\mathbf{len}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})})}
Elias-
\delta
code

(b)

Figure 13
:
Proof strategy for Theorem
2
.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of the lower bound. Let
f,g
satisfy the conditions for the minimization. The starting point for this proof is the classical result from information theory proved using Kraft’s inequality as follows.

Lemma 4

Let
\{l_{i}\}
be the codeword lengths of a binary code that is prefix-free. Assume a distribution over these codewords, and let
C
be a random codeword drawn according to that distribution. Then,

\displaystyle E_{C}\left[{\mathbf{len}(C)}\right]\geq H(C).

Using this result together with the assumption that the image of
f
is prefix-free, we can write

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right]=E_{f(\mathtt{S})}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right]\geq H(f(\mathtt{S}))\geq H(g(f(\mathtt{S})))\geq H({\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{S})))})=H({\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{S}})}),

(37)

where the last two inequalities follow from the fact that deterministic functions of random variables cannot increase entropy.

For any
\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}
such that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, we are assuming that
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s}))
,
g(f(\mathtt{s}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
and therefore using the definition of a Logic System, we have

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}\subseteq{\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{s})))}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}.

(38)

In particular, choosing
\mathtt{q}=\mathtt{s}
, we obtain
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{s})))}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
. This is geometrically described in Figure
13
-a, which illustrates the kernels of two queries satisfying
s\vdash q^{\prime}
,
s\vdash q^{\prime\prime}
(depicting the choice of
q
in the right hand side of (
38
)), as well as the conclusion that
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
. Substituting this in the right hand side of (
37
), we conclude

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S}))}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{1},\ldots,\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{|\mathcal{M}|})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{M}}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right),

where (a) already proves (
36
), (b) follows from the definition of
\vec{\kappa}(\cdot)
in (
15
), (c) follows from the independence assumption, and (d) follows from the
p_{s}
-law assumption combined with the “identically distributed” assumption. This completes the proof of the lower-bound result.

To prove the upper bound, we construct a code as follows. First, the sentence
\mathtt{s}
is mapped to its kernel,
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
; let
P_{K}
denote the probability distribution governing
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
. Then we use a Shannon code to encode this kernel, which uses a code length of

\displaystyle\left\lceil\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{P_{K}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})})}\right)\right\rceil\leq\log_{2}\left(\frac{1}{P_{K}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})})}\right)+1.

(39)

We point the reader to the early discussion of this quantity in (
4
). When substituting a random
\mathtt{S}
in lieu of
\mathtt{s}
, the expectation of the right hand side of the expectation of (
39
) is

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})+1.

(40)

One then writes

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{1},\ldots,\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{|\mathcal{M}|})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{M}}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{M}}H_{\text{bin}}\left(P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=1)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathcal{M}}P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=1)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right)=|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{s}),

where (a) follows from the definition of
\vec{\kappa}(\cdot)
in (
15
), (b) is a standard upper bound on the entropy of joint variables, (c) follows from the fact that
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}
is a binary random variable, (d) follows from the concavity of entropy, and (e) follows from the assumption of
\mathtt{S}
following a
p_{s}
-law.

We note that this result already proves the tighter upper bound of the Theorem and that such a tighter upper bound can be included in the results of our subsequent theorems, as explained in the discussion around (
17
).
We include an additional argument nonetheless, which only proves the looser upper bound, but that agrees in style with the rest of the results in the article; see Figure
13
. We first specify the encoder
f
. For a given size
\xi
of a kernel, there are a total of

\displaystyle{|\mathcal{M}|\choose\xi}\leq 2^{|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(\frac{\xi}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)}

possible kernels of the same size. Let
\mbox{enum}_{\xi}:\{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}:|k|=\xi\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{*}
be a function that maps each possible kernel of size
\xi
to a fixed-length binary encoding of the integers
\left\{1,\ldots,\displaystyle{|\mathcal{M}|\choose\xi}\right\}
, which is an integer that uniquely determines such a set.
Hence, in particular,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{enum}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}))}\leq|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)+1,

where in the above the binary entropy function
H_{\text{bin}}\left(p\right)=-p\log_{2}(p)-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)
is evaluated on the random variable
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|/|\mathcal{M}|
, and thus the result of the evaluation is also a random variable.

For a sentence kernel
\mathtt{s}\in\mathcal{L}
, we define the encoder
f
as a concatenation of two separate encodings:

\displaystyle f(\mathtt{s})=\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(|{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}|)\mbox{enum}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})}),

(41)

where the above is the result of the concatenation of two codes.
Since both of the codes implied by each encoding are prefix-free, the concatenation is also prefix-free. Finally, we let
g
be the decoder that recovers the kernel
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
from the output of
f
, and evaluates
\ell
on that kernel.

Note that, by construction, if
\mathtt{q}
is such that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, then since
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{s})))}
, we conclude
g(f(\mathtt{s}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
as well, and thus we have met the conditions of the Theorem.

The estimate for the overall cost of the encoding can be done by separately estimating the length of the two encodings in (
41
), namely

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)+\log_{2}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|+2\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\right)+4

bits. Taking the expectation with respect to
\mathtt{S}
, using the concavity
\cap
of the logarithm and entropy functions, and normalizing by
|\mathcal{M}|
, we obtain a normalized upper estimate of

\displaystyle H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right)+\frac{\log_{2}\left(p_{s}|\mathcal{M}|\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}\left(p_{s}|\mathcal{M}|\right)\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{4}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

Note that, in this upper bound, at no point did we assume the more restrictive condition involving the i.i.d. assumptions of the lower bound, thus completing the proof of the Theorem. This pattern of the upper bound holding under much more general conditions will repeat throughout our other proofs.
\hfill\square

5.2
Partial Ignorance

In this Theorem, both Alice and Bob have access to the same background logic sentence
\mathtt{R}
. It is important to emphasize that in this setup, such background information is
not
known at the initial meeting between Alice and Bob, and rather, it is the result of both independently collecting the same such logic sentences from the environment. We refer the reader to Figure
14
, which extends the corresponding Figure
13
by incorporating such background information. In some respects, the effect that the presence of the background
\mathtt{R}
has in the problem is rather elementary: we still want to send somehow
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, however we can now leverage the fact that both Alice and Bob know that such a kernel must be contained within
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
. In the same figure we show an updated strategy: both Alice and Bob first compute
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
, and then Alice uses it to enumerate all possible subsets of
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
of size
{\mathbf{len}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})}
, and sends
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|
followed by the index of the kernel she has in her possession. Bob then recovers
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
and uses
\ell
to recover a logic sentence.

Theorem 3
(Alice and Bob share a logic sentence)

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}\in\mathcal{L}
represent the sender’s logic sentence and the shared logic sentence, with the property that
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
and, in particular,
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{r}
)-law. Let the encoder
f
and decoder
g
be functions

\displaystyle f:\mathcal{L}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},

\displaystyle g:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}.

Then

\displaystyle\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}))}\right]\leq\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right),

where the minimization is over
f,g
such that the image of
f(\cdot,\mathtt{r})
is prefix-free for any choice of
\mathtt{r}
and such that, if
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{r}
and
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, then
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})
and
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})\vdash\mathtt{q}
. Furthermore if additionally the random variables
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))_{j}\}_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d. and
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})\leq\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}))}\right].

{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q^{\prime}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q^{\prime\prime}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}

(a)
\mathtt{r}
\kappa
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
\kappa
\mathtt{r}
\mathtt{s}
\kappa
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
ennumerative
code
decoder
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
\ell
\mathtt{\hat{s}}
{\mathbf{len}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})})}
Elias-
\delta
code

(b)

Figure 14
:
Proof strategy for Theorem
3
.

Proof.
As with Theorem
2
, we begin with the proof of the lower bound. Let
f,g
satisfy the conditions for the minimization. Paralleling the definition (
15
), for any given
k\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, let

\displaystyle k_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mbox{if }\mu_{j}\in k\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

For the proof of the lower bound, we will rely on the following Lemma, which is provided without proof since it is elementary.

Lemma 5

For given
0<p_{a}\leq p_{b}\leq 1
, let the random variables
A,B\in\{0,1\}
be such that
EA=p_{a}
and
EB=p_{b}
, and furthermore,
A\leq B
. Then conditioned on
B=1
, the random variable
A
is distributed according to
\{1-p_{a}/p_{b},p_{a}/p_{b}\}
.

We now derive

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

(43)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{R}}\left[E_{f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}))}|\mathtt{R}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H({\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}),\mathtt{R}))}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{S}})}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)\sum_{j}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{j}=k_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)\sum_{j:k_{j}=0}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{j}=0)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)\sum_{j:k_{j}=1}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{j}=1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(h)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)\sum_{j:k_{j}=1}H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{j}=1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\subseteq\mathcal{M}}P({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}=k)|k|H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}/p_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|p_{r}H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}/p_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s}),

where (a) follows from the law of total expectation, (b) follows from the assumption that the image of
f(\cdot,\mathtt{r})
is prefix-free for any choice of
\mathtt{r}
as well as the definition of conditional entropy, and (c) follows from the fact that deterministic functions of random quantities cannot increase entropy. To see (d),
note that
\mathtt{\hat{s}}=g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})
and by assumption
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{\hat{s}}
, and for any
\mathtt{q}
such that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, we have
\mathtt{\hat{s}}\vdash\mathtt{q}
. As a consequence, using the property (
6
) of a Logic System, we conclude

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}.

By choosing
\mathtt{q}=\mathtt{s}
, we obtain the statement that
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
. Since
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}={\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{S}})}
, the conditional distribution of either given
\mathtt{R}
is the same, establishing (d). Step (e) follows from the assumption that
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, and then utilizing the result (
7
). Step (f) follows from the assumption of independence. Step (g) is a simple splitting of the summation in (f). To justify (h), we use the assumption that
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
which in particular means that

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}.

Thus necessarily if
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{j}=0
then
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{j}=0
and the corresponding conditional entropy is zero. To justify (i), we invoke the “identically distributed” property, together with the assumption that
|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|=p_{s}
,
|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|=p_{r}
, as well as Lemma
5
. The final step (j) follows from the definition of
\Lambda
. This establishes the lower bound.

The upper bound is proved with a simple variant of the proof of Theorem
2
, where
\mathcal{M}
is substituted with
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
. We write the proof here for completeness. For a given size
\xi
of a kernel, there are a total of

\displaystyle{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|\choose\xi}\leq 2^{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(\frac{\xi}{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}\right)}

possible kernels of the same size, since both sender and receiver share knowledge of
\mathtt{R}
. Let
\mbox{enum}_{\xi,\psi}:\{k_{s}\subseteq\mathcal{M}:|k_{s}|=\xi\}\times\{k_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{M}:|k_{r}|=\psi\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{*}
be a function that maps each possible pair
(k_{s},k_{r})
of kernels respectively of size
\xi
and
\psi
such that
k_{s}\subseteq k_{r}
, to a fixed-length binary encoding of the integers

\left\{1,\ldots,\displaystyle{\psi\choose\xi}\right\}
,
which is an integer that uniquely determines
k_{s}
as a subset of
k_{r}
. Then, in particular,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{enum}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})},{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|H_{\text{bin}}\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}\right)+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda(|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|-|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|)+1.

The last step follows from the definition of
\Lambda
; note that the resulting expression is a random variable. For given sentences
\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}\in\mathcal{L}
, we define the encoder
f
as a concatenation of two separate encodings:

\displaystyle f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r})=\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(|{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}|)\mbox{enum}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{s})},{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}).

(44)

Since both of the codes implied by each encoding are prefix-free, the concatenation is also prefix-free. Finally, we let
g
be the decoder that recovers the kernel
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
from the output of
f
, and evaluates
\ell
on that kernel.

We note that, by construction, if
\mathtt{q}
is such that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, then
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})\vdash\mathtt{q}
since
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r}))}
, and thus we have met the conditions of the Theorem. The estimate for the overall cost of the encoding can be done by separately estimating the length of the two encodings in (
44
), namely

\displaystyle\Lambda(|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|-|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|)+\log_{2}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|+2\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\right)+4.

Taking the expectation with respect to
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}
, using the concavity
\cap
of
\Lambda
(see Lemma
2
) as well as of the logarithm, and normalizing by
|\mathcal{M}|
, we obtain an upper estimate of

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})+\frac{\log_{2}\left(p_{s}|\mathcal{M}|\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}\left(p_{s}|\mathcal{M}|\right)\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{4}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

As before, the upper bounds holds under the more general assumption that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{r}
)-law. This completes the proof of the Theorem.

5.3
The partition compression problem

In this subsection, we treat a lossy data compression problem of central relevance to the “targeted query” settings of Theorems
5
,
7
and
8
. We refer the reader to Figure
15
. Imagine one has
n
items as well as two non-intersecting subsets of those
n
items, which we call
A
and
B
, and one is interested in efficiently sending to a receiver a subset
M
which contains
A
but excludes
B
; or, alternatively stated, a partition that separates
A
from
B
. One solution is to send
M=A
or
M=B
, whichever is cheapest to send, but it turns out that there is generally a better solution. Let
X^{n}\in\{0,1,2\}^{n}
be a vector with
X_{i}=1
if the
i
th element is inside of
A
,
X_{i}=0
if the
i
th element is inside of
B
, and
X_{i}=2
if the
i
th element is neither in
A
nor in
B
. Assume that the entries of
X^{n}
meet the following conditions:

\displaystyle n^{-1}E|\{i:X_{i}=0\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{b},

\displaystyle n^{-1}E|\{i:X_{i}=1\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{a},

\displaystyle n^{-1}E|\{i:X_{i}=2\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-p_{a}-p_{b}.

(45)

B
M
A

Figure 15
:
An example of a partition
\{M,M^{c}\}
that separates
A
from
B
.

Define a distortion metric to be a function

\rho:\{0,1,2\}\times\{0,1\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}

(46)

using this matrix

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}&0&1\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1\\
1&1&0\\
2&0&0\end{array}\;\;.

(47)

An encoder and decoder are functions
f_{n}:\{0,1,2\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{*}
and
g_{n}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, respectively. The problem is to find a good upper bound for

\displaystyle\min_{f_{n},g_{n}}n^{-1}E_{X^{n}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f_{n}(X^{n}))}\right]

subject to the condition that, for any
x_{1}^{n}\in\{0,1,2\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\rho(x_{i},g_{n}(f_{n}(x_{1}^{n}))_{i})=0.

(48)

The solution to this problem can be obtained by an application of Shannon’s Rate-Distortion theory.

Theorem 4
(Shannon bounds for partition compression)

For any
n\geq 1
, let
X_{1}^{n}\in\{0,1,2\}^{n}
be a random vector with the property (
45
). Let
f_{n},g_{n}
be encoder and decoder functions as defined earlier. Then

\displaystyle\min_{f_{n},g_{n}}n^{-1}E_{X}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f_{n}(X_{1}^{n}))}\right]\leq\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(n\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})\right)}{n}+\frac{3}{n},

where the minimization is over
f_{n},g_{n}
, such that the image of
f_{n}
is prefix-free and that condition (
48
) is met. Furthermore, if the random variables
\{X_{i}\}
are additionally
i.i.d., we have

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})\leq\min_{f_{n},g_{n}}n^{-1}E_{X}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f_{n}(X_{1}^{n}))}\right].

Remark:
From Lemma
2
, if
p_{a}+p_{b}<1
, then we can deduce that
\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})<\min\{H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{a}\right),H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{b}\right)\}
, and thus this result predicts the existence of partition compression techniques which are more efficient than the “naive” solution of sending the cheapest of the sets (
A
or
B
).

Proof.

We provide a proof of the upper bound that is simple and includes a useful second order upper bound which will play a role in the proofs of Theorems
5
,
7
. As we will show, the expected performance of a random matrix with the density of ones appropriately tuned will asymptotically approach the Shannon limit
\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})
. This implies, as per the classic random coding argument of Shannon, the existence of a deterministic code with performance at least as good as the expected performance of the random code.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\{0,1,2\}^{n}
be the input random vector, and
let
\Psi\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
denote the random positions where
X_{i}
is taking on
0
or
1
,
thus where we want to enforce a bit pattern. For any arbitrary
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
[z]_{\Psi}
denote the
1\times|\Psi|
vector obtained by extracting from
z
the columns indexed by
\Psi
.

Let
C
be a random binary matrix with an infinite number of rows
\{C_{1},C_{2},\ldots\}
and each with
n
columns. Assume its entries are chosen i.i.d. according to the distribution

\displaystyle P(C_{i,j}=0)=\frac{p_{a}}{p_{a}+p_{b}}.

The sender scans the matrix
C
from top to bottom until it finds the first row
J
that satisfies the following condition:

\displaystyle[C_{J}]_{\Psi}=X_{\Psi},

(49)

and sends the index
J
of that row. The receiver then recovers the row from the index. Let
N_{0}=\{i:X_{i}=0\}
and
N_{1}=\{i:X_{i}=1\}
. The probability of a row of
C
satisfying (
49
), conditional on
\Psi
, is given by

\displaystyle P([C_{i}]_{\Psi}=X_{\Psi}|\Psi)=\left(\frac{p_{a}}{p_{a}+p_{b}}\right)^{N_{0}}\left(\frac{p_{b}}{p_{a}+p_{b}}\right)^{N_{1}}.

Note that independence of the entries in the vector
X
was not necessary to assert this, and instead, the way
C
is constructed is sufficient.
It is now easy to calculate

P(J=j)=(1-P([C_{i}]_{\Psi}=X_{\Psi}|\Psi))^{j-1}P([C_{i}]_{\Psi}=X_{\Psi}|\Psi)

since
J
is the
first
occurrence of the pattern
\Psi
.
As a consequence,

\displaystyle E\left[J|\Psi\right]=1/P([C_{i}]_{\Psi}=X_{\Psi}|\Psi)=2^{\left(-N_{0}\log_{2}\frac{p_{a}}{p_{a}+p_{b}}-N_{1}\log_{2}\frac{p_{b}}{p_{a}+p_{b}}\right)}.

To send the index
J
we will be using
\delta
Elias coding. Note that
E_{\Psi}\left[\log_{2}\left(E\left[J|\Psi\right]\right)\right]=n\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})
. We can then upper bound the performance of the code as

\displaystyle E_{J}\left[{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(J))}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\Psi}\left[E_{J}\left[{\mathbf{len}(\mbox{elias}_{\delta}(J))}|\Psi\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E_{\Psi}\left[E_{J}\left[\log_{2}J+2\log_{2}(\log_{2}J)+3\ |\Psi\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E_{\Psi}\left[\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]+2\log_{2}(\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right])+3\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E_{\Psi}\left[\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right]\right]+2\log_{2}(E_{\Psi}\left[\log_{2}E_{J}\left[J|\Psi\right])+3\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b})+2\log_{2}(n\Lambda(p_{a},p_{b}))+3,

where in addition to the estimate of the performance of
\delta
Elias coding, we used the concavity
\cap
of the logarithm. The lower bound, which is only valid in the case
X_{1}^{n}
is an i.i.d. random vector, is a straightforward consequence of existing
R(D)
literature
[
12
]
so it is omitted.

5.4
Less is more

In this pattern the purpose of Alice is not to communicate to Bob enough to prove anything she can prove; rather it is to communicate to him the minimal amount of information needed to prove a
specific sentence

\mathtt{q}
(and of course, having achieved that, any other sentence that is entailed by
\mathtt{q}
). We refer the reader to the top of Figure
16
. In here we illustrate
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
in green, and
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
in white. One fairly obvious strategy to solve this problem would be to send to Bob enough information to reconstruct
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
; then Bob will be able to prove anything that can be proved starting from
\mathtt{q}
. Alternately, one could send to Bob enough information to reconstruct
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
itself, which would allow Bob to prove potentially even more logic sentences. For the purposes of quantifying the cost of either of these two strategies, let us assume that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{q}
)-law (with
p_{s}<p_{q}
), then using the ideas behind Theorem
2
, and choosing the best from either of these two strategies, we would be spending a normalized average total of

\displaystyle\min\{H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{s}\right),H_{\text{bin}}\left(p_{q}\right)\}

(50)

bits (neglecting asymptotically vanishing terms). Yet, as we will soon show, this strategy is in general
suboptimal
. This was a surprising revelation to us, so we want to equip the reader with the insight used to prove this result. From the top of Figure
16
, it should be apparent that not only could Alice use either of the two strategies above to communicate to Bob; in fact she has the freedom to send any possible
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
that satisfies
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
; one such example
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
is illustrated in blue in the Figure. However, crucially, note that a set
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
that is bigger than
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
, even though it may feel more complex to describe, can actually be good for many choices for
\mathtt{s}
and
\mathtt{q}
,
and thus we may not need that big a pre-agreed collection of those
. This insight means we can beat the estimate (
50
). In the same figure, we show an architecture with a special form of encoding which was introduced in the proof of Theorem
4
.

Theorem 5
(Goal is a targeted query)

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}\in\mathcal{L}
represent the sender’s and query logic sentences, respectively, with the property that
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
and in particular,
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{q}
)-law. Let the encoder
f
and decoder
g
be functions

\displaystyle f:\mathcal{L}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},

\displaystyle g:\{0,1\}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}.

Then

\displaystyle\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))}\right]\leq\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right),

where the minimization is over
f,g
such that the image of
f
is prefix-free and such that, if
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, then
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))
and
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
. If additionally,
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}))_{j}\}_{j=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d., then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})\leq\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))}\right]

\mathtt{Q}
\kappa^{c}
\mathtt{S}
\kappa
+
-
enc
partition
decode
\ell
\mathtt{\hat{S}}

{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}

(a)

(b)

Figure 16
:
Proof strategy for Theorem
5

Proof.
As with Theorem
2
, we start by invoking Kraft’s inequality through Lemma
4
and write

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))}\right]\geq H(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))\geq H(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))).

The direction of the proof now diverges with respect to that of Theorem
2
, by deriving

\displaystyle H(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q})))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q})))-H(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle I(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q};g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q})))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))))

where (a) follows from the fact that discrete entropy is zero when conditioning on all randomness, (b) is from the definition of mutual information, and (c) follows from the data processing inequality.

Referencing Figure
16
, we now construct a function
\psi
that accepts
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})
and
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})
and produces a vector of length
|\mathcal{M}|
with entries in
\{0,1,2\}
indicating whether each entry is in the red (0), green (1) or white (2) regions. Let
\mathtt{v},\mathtt{w}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
. The construction is as follows:

\displaystyle\psi(\mathtt{v},\mathtt{w})=0\times(\underline{1}-\mathtt{w})+1\times\mathtt{v}+2\times(\underline{1}-\mathtt{v})\mathtt{w},

(51)

where the
\times
operator is multiplying a scalar times a vector element-wise, and
\underline{1}
represents a vector of all ones, and the product of vectors in the last term is element-wise. Of course, the leftmost term is always zero, but we included it to ensure that the connection to Figure
16
is clear. Next define

\displaystyle X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})),

\displaystyle Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\times\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))).

The relationship between
X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
and
Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
is in general very complex, as we have few assumptions on
f
and
g
. However, some key assertions can be made. First, recall that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{q}
)-law; see Definition
3
, and in particular recall that
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
. Using elementary probability,

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=0])+P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0])-P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=0]\cup[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0])

where the last equality uses (
6
) from the fundamental Definition
1
, to conclude that
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})
where the inequality is to be interpreted element-wise. Now using the “identically distributed” assumption from the (and not yet independence), we obtain

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0)=1-p_{q}

With a far simpler argument, also without using independence and only the identical distributed assumption, we obtain

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1)=P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=1])=p_{s}

Finally we add the independence assumption, thus concluding that the
\{X_{i}\}
are i.i.d. according to the distribution

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-p_{q},

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{s},

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{q}-p_{s}.

To continue, the assumption that
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))
,
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
can be used to establish a useful relation between
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
. Recall the definition of the distortion metric
\rho
from Equations (
46
) and (
47
). Then, the assumption implies that, for all
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{M}|
,

\displaystyle\rho(X_{i},Z_{i})=0.

(52)

This is an important fact that will be used shortly. We now apply the data processing inequality once more, taking advantage of the definitions for
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
, and continue the proof with a pattern commonly found in Rate-Distortion theory:

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle I(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|})

(53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|})-H(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}H(X_{i})-H(X_{i}|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|},X_{1},\ldots X_{i-1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}H(X_{i})-H(X_{i}|Z_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}I(X_{i};Z_{i})

where (d) follows from the data processing inequality, (e) follows from the fact that the
\{X_{i}\}
are independent and from the chain rule for entropy, and (f) follows from the fact that conditioning cannot increase entropy.

We now pause to observe that for any given random variables
V,W
, the mutual information
I(V;W)
is an expression that can be entirely computed from
p_{V}
and
Q_{W|V}
as these two completely determine the distribution of
V,W
. Thus we could write
I(V,W)=\iota(p_{V},Q_{W|V})
for some function
\iota
and in particular the step (
53
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\iota(p_{X_{i}},Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\iota(p_{X_{1}},Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i}})

(54)

where this last equality follows from the fact that
the marginal for
X_{i}
is identical for all
i
, but the conditionals
Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i}}
are in general different. It is known that the function
\iota(p_{V},Q_{W|V})
is convex
\cup
on
Q_{W|V}
and therefore by Jensen’s inequality, the expression (
54
) can be lower bounded by

\displaystyle\iota\left(p_{X_{1}},\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i}}(z|x)\right).

Let
X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
be distributed according to the marginal for
X_{1}
and the averaging of conditional distribution above. Because of (
52
), it is the case that

\displaystyle E_{X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}}\rho(X^{\prime},Z^{\prime})=0.

Therefore the following bound holds

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}I(X_{i};Z_{i})\geq|\mathcal{M}|I(X^{\prime};Z^{\prime})\geq|\mathcal{M}|\min_{P_{X,Z}\in\mathcal{D}}I(X;Z),

where the domain
\mathcal{D}
for the minimization is defined by joint distributions for
X,Z
with
X\sim(1-p_{q},p_{s},p_{q}-p_{s})
and
E[\rho(X,Z)]=0
. The fact that such a minimization results in
\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})
can be checked using standard variational methods. This concludes the proof of the lower bound.

To prove the upper bound, we construct a code as follows. Define

\displaystyle X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})).

We note that by construction, under the weaker assumption that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
have kernels that follow a
(p_{s},p_{q})
-law.

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}P(X_{i}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-p_{q},

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}P(X_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{s},

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}P(X_{i}=2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{q}-p_{s}.

In reference to (
45
), we next invoke the upper bound of Theorem
4
, which guarantees the existence of
\hat{f}
,
\hat{g}
such that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(\hat{f}(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}))}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{3}{|\mathcal{M}|},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\rho(X_{i},\hat{g}(\hat{f}(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}))_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

We define our encoder then as

\displaystyle f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q})=\hat{f}(\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q}))).

Recall that
\hat{g}(\mbox{codeword})\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
and thus it can be regarded also as a kernel via the dual notation where kernels are depicted by the indicator vector of the corresponding subset of
\mathcal{M}
. To conclude, we define the decoder
g
as

\displaystyle g(\mbox{codeword})=\vec{\ell}(\hat{g}(\mbox{codeword})),

where
\mbox{codeword}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
is meant to be precisely
f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q})
when the encoder and decoder are being used simultaneously. We conclude the proof by noting that, by construction, if
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
, we have
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))
,
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
and furthermore

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}))}\right]\leq\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})+2\frac{\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s},1-p_{q})\right)}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{3}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

\hfill\square

5.5
No need to know

In this problem, Bob’s logic sentence
\mathtt{R}
is unavailable to Alice, and the goal of the communication is for Bob to be able to prove anything that Alice can. To address the problem, we will need to introduce a more complex communication pattern, where Alice and Bob are able to exchange messages over several rounds, taking turns on who is sending and who is receiving. As before, we will use
f
to denote an encoder and
g
to denote a decoder, but the agent doing the encoding and decoding will change depending on the turn.

Table 1
:
A 4-turn code for full synchronization. It is assumed that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{r}
. The actual step of Bob proving queries is not shown.

Table
1
is an exemplary representation of the conversational paradigm that we are now entering. In this table, the context to which either Alice or Bob are privy is shown, split into whether the context is either common or private. The context informs what the encoder or decoder functions are allowed to depend on; in addition, every decoder is allowed to depend on the output of the corresponding encoder, which is denoted by the letter on the top of each arrow indicating the direction of the communication. For example, the
f^{3}
encoder, whose output is
a^{3}
, may depend on
\mathtt{s},b^{0},a^{1}
and
b^{2}
. In all cases, the image for each of the
f^{i}
encoders is a subset of
\{0,1\}^{*}
. The communication task finishes when
g^{3}
computes its output
\mathtt{\hat{s}_{m}}\in\mathcal{L}
which can now be used to prove any
\mathtt{q}
with the property that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
. To measure the efficiency of communication, we define the total normalized average cost:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}\right]

where
B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3}
are random versions of
b^{0},a^{1},b^{2},a^{3}
when in the interaction the sender experiences
\mathtt{S}
and the receiver experiences
\mathtt{R}
.
The reader may recall that in previous results, we assumed that the image of an encoder was a prefix-free code. In a similar manner, we assume that the image of encoder
f^{0}
is prefix-free. In turn 1, both parties have
b^{0}
as common context, and therefore the assumption that we have is that the image of
f^{1}
, when the value of
b^{0}
is kept fixed, is prefix-free. Similarly, we assume that the image of
f^{2}
, when the value of
b^{0},a^{1}
are kept fixed, is prefix-free and finally, we assume that the image of
f^{3}
when
b^{0},a^{1},b^{2}
are kept fixed is prefix-free. We call any code with the properties illustrated by Table
1
and this discussion a
4-turn code
for full synchronization.

Before presenting the result we introduce the main intuition behind how the upper bound of this result is proved. Alice and Bob meet ahead of time, and they agree on a family of hash functions that can be used to map sets
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
of various cardinalities to a bin in a many-to-one fashion. After the receiver is presented with
\mathtt{R}
and the sender is presented with
\mathtt{S}
, in Turn 0, the receiver informs the sender of the cardinality of
\mathtt{R}
set, and as part of Turn 1, the sender does the same but for
\mathtt{S}
. As a result of this calibration, also as part of Turn 1, the sender sends the index of a hash bin where
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
has been mapped. Aided by
\mathtt{R}
, the receiver is able to recover
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
from the hash bin the majority of time. Turns 2 and 3 are there to address the possible exception where the receiver fails to recover
\kappa(\mathtt{S})
in Turn 1. The communication costs in Turns 0, 2 and 3 are asymptotically negligible and thus Turn 1 dominates the total communication cost. The main task is to demonstrate that asymptotically, the normalized logarithm of the number of such bins approaches
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})
and to demonstrate that all other communication cost is negligible.

{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
