Figure 17
:
Illustration of the general strategy for the upper bound in the proof of Theorem
6
, based on sending the index of a hash bin containing the kernel of the sender’s sentence, and then resolving ambiguity by testing whether
\mathtt{R}
is entailed.

Theorem 6
(Alice doesn’t know what Bob knows, a.k.a. No Need to Know)

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}\in\mathcal{L}
represent the sender’s logic sentence and the receiver’s logic sentence, assuming
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{r}
)-law (and thus
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
). Then

\displaystyle\min_{\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{3}}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right).

where the minimization is over all 4-turn codes for full synchronization, as defined by Table
1
and the corresponding explanation of it. Furthermore, the if
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d., and furthermore,
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{3}}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}\right].

Proof.

The lower bound is proved as follows. We start from from a general 4-turn code for full synchronization
\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{3}
, and let
B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
be the binary strings output by the encoders
f^{0},f^{1},f^{2},f^{3}
that are used by Bob and Alice, when the interaction is operating over the random quantities
\mathtt{S}
and
\mathtt{R}
. The expected communication cost is then lower bounded as follows:

\displaystyle E\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle E\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}\right]+E\left[E\left[{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}|B^{0}\right]\right]

(55)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle E\left[E\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}|B^{0},A^{1}\right]\right]+E\left[E\left[{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}|B^{0},A^{1},B^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H(B^{0})+H(A^{1}|B^{0})+H(B^{2}|B^{0},A^{1})+H(A^{3}|B^{0},A^{1},B^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H(B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H(\mathtt{\hat{S}}|\mathtt{R}).

Step (a) is a straightforward application of the law of total expectation. Step (b) follows from the application of Lemma
4
and the prefix-free assumption in the theorem.
The argument continues as follows:

\displaystyle H(\mathtt{\hat{S}}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{S}})}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H({\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s}).

Step (c) is justified using arguments similar to those of Subsection
5.2
substituting
\mathtt{\hat{s}}=g^{3}(\mathtt{r},b^{0},a^{1},b^{2},a^{3})
instead. Step (d) is justified by exactly the same derivation found in (
43
), starting from step (e). This establishes the lower bound.

We now turn our attention to the upper bound. First we establish the precise domain/image of the encoder and decoders for our 4-turn mode:

\displaystyle f^{0}:\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{0}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle f^{1}:\mathcal{L}\times\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{1}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\times\{\text{success},\text{failure}\},

\displaystyle f^{2}:\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{2}:\{0,1\}\rightarrow\{\text{success},\text{failure}\},

\displaystyle f^{3}:\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{3}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}\times\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}.

Alice and Bob first interact to establish a family of hash functions. How this is done will be described shortly. The transmission protocol is as follows:

1.

The receiver sends
|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|
to the sender using

\delta
Elias coding, which is decoded at the sender side. This step defines
f^{0}
and
g^{0}
.

2.

The sender sends
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|
to the receiver using
\delta
Elias coding, which is decoded at the receiver side. This only partly defines
f^{1}
and
g^{1}
.

3.

At this point, the receiver and sender independently can compute

\displaystyle\text{RATE}=\Lambda\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|},\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}-\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)+\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

The same hash function is then selected by both parties independently, which matches the selected rate and depends on
\mathcal{M}
,
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|
and
|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|
.

4.

The sender sends the bin index
\text{BIN}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})
to the receiver using
2^{|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}
bits. This step completes the definition of
f^{1}
.

5.

The receiver attempts to decode
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
using the bin index, its knowledge of
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
and the algorithm described below. By leveraging the function
\ell
, the outcome of this attempt results in an element of
\mathcal{L}
and a “success” or a default, dummy element from
\mathcal{L}
and a “failure”. This completes the definition of
g^{1}
.

6.

The success/failure of the attempt is signaled back to the sender using a single bit. This defines both
f^{2}
and
g^{2}
.

7.

If successful, the sender has nothing to do anymore, as the receiver expects no further communication. The receiver simply outputs the element from
\mathcal{L}
computed by
g^{1}
, designating it as the output
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
, partly defining
g^{3}
.

8.

If unsuccessful, the sender sends
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
as a binary vector of length
|\mathcal{M}|
, which is decoded by the receiver and by passing it through
\ell
, becomes
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
, the designated output of the protocol, completing the definition of
f^{3}
and
g^{3}
.

The reader may appreciate that the algorithm guarantees that the receiver at the end will have in possession enough information to reproduce a message
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
that is functionally equivalent to
\mathtt{S}
. The problem that remains is demonstrating that the overall communication efficiency of this protocol is such that the normalized total bidirectional cost in bits is asymptotically
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})
.

{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
\text{BIN}(\mathtt{S})

Figure 18
:
An example of a hash bin collision in Theorem
6
, where the receiver is unable to decide between the true kernel in yellow and a confounding one in red.

Prior to stating the protocol we disclosed that Alice and Bob met before to agree on a family of hash functions. We describe next how this agreement is arrived to. For each possible combination of potential sender and receiver kernel sizes
0\leq s\leq r\leq|\mathcal{M}|
with corresponding coding rate
\mbox{rate}(s,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\Lambda\left(\frac{s}{|\mathcal{M}|},\frac{r}{|\mathcal{M}|}-\frac{s}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)+\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}
, we will build a random hash function for
\{\mathtt{s}\in\mathcal{L}:|{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}|=s\}
. For each element of this set we choose a bin index independently and uniformly at random from the set
\{1,\ldots,\lceil 2^{|\mathcal{M}|\cdot\mbox{rate}(s,r)}\rceil\}
; call the resulting function
\text{BIN}_{s,r}:\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots,\lceil 2^{|\mathcal{M}|\cdot\mbox{rate}(s,r)}\rceil\}
. We use the upper case notation BIN to remind the reader that this is a random hash function. The expected, normalized cost for the transmissions associated with steps
1
,
2
,
4
admit the following upper bounds, obtained using convexity arguments and the assumption that
(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{r}
)-law:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\left(\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|p_{s}\right)+2\log_{2}\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|p_{s}\right)+3\right),

(56)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\left(\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|p_{r}\right)+2\log_{2}\log_{2}\left(|\mathcal{M}|p_{r}\right)+3\right),

(57)

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})+\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

(58)

The last term uses Lemma
2
. We now describe the decoding algorithm behind step
5
. As of step
2
, the receiver has decoded
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|
and step
4
, the receiver has decoded the bin index
\text{BIN}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})
. Next he would like to recover
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
itself. Recall that by assumption,
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R}
and therefore the receiver knows that irrespective of what
\mathtt{S}
is, the following relation must hold:

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}.

Define the hypotheses set as

\displaystyle\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\left\{\alpha\subseteq\mathcal{M}:\alpha\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})},|\alpha|=|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,\text{BIN}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}(\alpha)=\text{BIN}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}({\kappa(\mathtt{S})})\right\}.

If the hypotheses set has cardinality exactly one,
g^{1}
outputs the result of mapping the one element to the space of logic sentences using
\ell
and additionally outputs “success”; here
\ell
refers to the function that maps a kernel to a logic expression (c.f. Definition
28
). Otherwise,
g^{1}
outputs a dummy element from
\mathcal{L}
(doesn’t matter which) and additionally outputs “failure”. We illustrate an example of a decoding failure in Figure
18
. The box in white represents
\text{BIN}_{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|,|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}(\mathtt{S})
, and the box in gray represents
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
. The various circles represent kernels that have been mapped to the same bin. The yellow circle represents the true kernel
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
that we want Bob to recover. The kernels illustrated through the green color can be eliminated by the receiver from consideration, since they are not fully included in
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
. The kernel illustrated in red cannot be discerned from the kernel in yellow by the receiver, and thus in this example, we have a decoding failure.

To estimate the failure probability, we upper bound the probability of this event:

\displaystyle\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right].

Let
I_{\alpha}
be equal to 1 if
\alpha\in\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})
and 0 otherwise. Then we can upper bound the error probability as

\displaystyle P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{\alpha}I_{\alpha}\geq 2\right)

(59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}}\left[P\left(\sum_{\alpha}I_{\alpha}\geq 2\lvert\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}\right)\right].

We then write

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{\alpha}I_{\alpha}\geq 2\lvert\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-P\left(\sum_{\alpha:\alpha\neq{\kappa(\mathtt{S})},\alpha\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})},|\alpha|=|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}I_{\alpha}=0\lvert\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R}\right)

(60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{\alpha\neq{\kappa(\mathtt{S})},\alpha\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})},|\alpha|=|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}P(I_{\alpha}=0|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{\alpha\neq{\kappa(\mathtt{S})},\alpha\subseteq{\kappa(\mathtt{R})},|\alpha|=|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}\left(1-2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1-\left(1-2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}\right)^{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|\\
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\end{array}\right)}.

We describe the rationale behind this critical derivation. By construction, the hypotheses set always contains at least one element (the true kernel for the sentence in possession by Alice), and thus the first equality (
60
) focuses on estimating the failure probability by estimating instead the success probability, where every kernel other than
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
is not mapped to the hypotheses set. The second equality (
61
) follows by recognizing that under the given conditioning, the terms identified in the summation are statistically independent and hence the overall probability can be reduced to a product of individual probabilities. The third equality uses the specifics on how we constructed the random hash function to compute the probability that a kernel is not mapped to the bin to which
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
belongs. Finally (
5.5
) uses combinatorial counting arguments to obtain an estimate of the product. We bound the combinatorial as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|\\
|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{|\mathcal{M}|\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}H\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|}\right)}

(68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda\left(\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|},\frac{|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|-|{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}-\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}.

Recall now Bernoulli’s inequality:

\displaystyle(1-a)^{n}\geq 1-an.

Combining this inequality with (
5.5
), (
68
), and (
59
), we obtain

\displaystyle P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

With this estimate, we can account for the remainder of the bits in the protocol (going on either direction) with the expression

\displaystyle\left(1+P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right)|\mathcal{M}|\right)/|\mathcal{M}|\leq 2/|\mathcal{M}|.

Using this estimate together with (
56
), (
57
), (
58
), we obtain that the cost in bits is at most

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{s})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right).

The proof of the upper bound is completed by noting that since the above is the expected performance of a random hash function, there must exist at least one hash function with an error probability not worse than the average.
\hfill\square

5.6
Bob’s sentence may not be entailed by Alice’s

In Figure
19
-a, we illustrate the same situation that we had described in Theorem
5
, but with the addition of background information
\mathtt{r}
that is known to both Bob and Alice, and with the assumption
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{r}
. Now suppose that it is no longer the case that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{r}
, then what we obtain is the more general setup in Figure
19
, where as it can be appreciated,
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
no longer contains
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
fully and where the complement of
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
is patterned with a “dotted” fill. This more general setup is the subject of this subsection.

In order to derive results for this setting, we will need to make additional assumptions. For the first time in the article, we will use a
proper
Logic System (see Definition
2
), where we assume the availability of the standard logic operators
\lor
,
\land
,
\lnot
as well as the set theoretic implications of the kernels resulting from such operations; see Equations (
1
-
3
).
We introduce a more complex set of measurements that need to be made on the probability laws of
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
. We keep the assumption
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
in (
10
) but drop the assumptions
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{R},\mathtt{Q}\vdash\mathtt{R}
in (
9
),(
11
).
We measure the following expected kernel sizes:

\displaystyle p_{r}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|,

\displaystyle p_{s^{*}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})}|,

\displaystyle p_{q^{*}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})}|,

\displaystyle p_{s^{**}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})}|,

\displaystyle p_{q^{**}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})}|.

Our upper bounds will be phrased in terms of these expected sizes. Together with the assumption that
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
, we say that the kernels of
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
follow a (
p_{r},p_{s^{*}},p_{q^{*}},p_{s^{**}},p_{q^{**}}
)-law. As with previous results, we briefly introduce the strategy for proving the upper bound for this result is illustrated in Figure
19
. While the figure appears formidable, upon further examination its elements are quickly decomposed into elements that should be familiar to the reader now. At the highest level, the problem is simply split in two: because both Alice and Bob know
\mathtt{r}
, they can create a dual strategy: one to handle sending whatever piece of
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
and
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
that will intersect with
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
, and the other one to handle the same but that intersects with
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}^{c}
; this explains why there is vertical symmetry on the figure. Then, focusing on, say, only the top half of the diagram, we realize that the resulting system is in essence a combination of the strategies used in Theorems
3
and
5
. As a result, the fundamental device for efficiently sending partitions, denoted by a circle with the
+
and
-
hooks (and fully addressed in Theorem
4
) is used twice.

Theorem 7
(Bob’s sentence may not be entailed by Alice’s)

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a proper Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S}
,
\mathtt{Q}
,
\mathtt{R}
represent the sender, query and receiver logic sentences, respectively, which we assume have kernels that follow a (
p_{r},p_{s^{*}},p_{q^{*}},p_{s^{**}},p_{q^{**}}
)-law. Let the encoder
f
and decoder
g
be functions

\displaystyle f:\mathcal{L}^{3}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*}

\displaystyle g:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}.

Then

\displaystyle\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s^{*}},p_{r}-p_{q^{*}})+\Lambda(p_{s^{**}},1-p_{r}-p_{q^{**}})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)

where the minimization is over
f,g
such that the image of
f(\cdot,\cdot,\mathtt{r})
is prefix-free for any choice of
\mathtt{r}
, and such that if
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
then
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})
, and
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})\vdash\mathtt{q}
. Furthermore, if
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d. and
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow({\kappa(\mathtt{S})},{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})})
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s^{*}},p_{r}-p_{q^{*}})+\Lambda(p_{s^{**}},1-p_{r}-p_{q^{**}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{f,g}|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}^{c}
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{\hat{s}})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}

(a)                     (b)
\mathtt{r}
\kappa
\kappa
\mathtt{r}
\mathtt{q}
\kappa^{c}
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
shortened
{\kappa(\mathtt{q})}^{c}
-indicator
\mathtt{s}
\kappa
{\kappa(\mathtt{r})}
shortened
{\kappa(\mathtt{s})}
-indicator
+

+

\mathtt{q}

\mathtt{r}

(c)

Proof strategy for Theorem

Proof.

The proof of this result builds upon the ideas in the proofs of Theorem
3
and
5
. We start using the law of total expectation to write

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}\right]=E_{\mathtt{R}}\left[E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}|\mathtt{R}\right]\right]

We invoke again Kraft’s inequality through Lemma
4
and write

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}))}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r})|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}))}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle H(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r})|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

where we have used the assumption that
f(\cdot,\cdot,\mathtt{r})
is prefix-free for any choice of
\mathtt{r}
. From here, we apply the same ideas as in Theorem
5
to obtain

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}))}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}\right]\geq I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r}))|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r}).

Recall the definition of
\psi
in (
51
) and write

\displaystyle X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})),

\displaystyle Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\times\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})).

Similarly, as before note that the assumptions that
\mathtt{s}\vdash g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r})
and
\mathtt{r})
,
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r})\vdash\mathtt{q}
imply that for all
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{M}|
,

\displaystyle\rho(X_{i},Z_{i})=0.

We continue applying the ideas in the proof of Theorem
5
and obtain

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r}))|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle I(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})-H(Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|},\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))-H(Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|},\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))-H(Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

where (a) follows from the assumption that the following Markov chain holds:

\displaystyle\mathtt{R}\rightarrow\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\rightarrow(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})),

and (b) follows from the fact that conditional entropy can only increase under weaker conditioning.

We will next argue that conditioned on
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})
, the
\{X_{i}\}
are independent, and distributed according to at most two distributions, which follow from the assumptions of the distribution of
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{R},\mathtt{Q}
.

Lemma 6

Let the random variables
A,B,C\in\{0,1\}
and assume that
A\leq B
. Then the distribution of
\psi(A,B)
given
C=c

\displaystyle\left\{P(B=0|C=c),P(A=1|C=c),1-P(B=0|C=c)-P(A=1|C=c)\right\}.

(69)

Proof.
Recall that

\displaystyle\psi(A,B)=A+2(1-A)B

and therefore, the the following event equivalences hold:

\displaystyle\left[\psi(A,B)=1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[A=1\right]

\displaystyle\left[\psi(A,B)=0\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[A=0,B=0\right]=\left[B=0\right]

where the last equality follows from the assumption that
A\leq B
. Then given
C=c
,

\displaystyle P(\psi(A,B)=1|C=c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(A=1|C=c)

\displaystyle P(\psi(A,B)=0|C=c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(B=0|C=c)

\hfill\square

We now write

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))_{i}=0|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1)&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1\\
P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0)&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r}&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1\\
1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r})&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0\end{array}\right.

where (a) follows from the Definition
X_{i}=\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i})
and the independence assumption and (b) follows from Lemma
6
.
To go from (b) to (c) we establish that
{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}={\kappa((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))}
using the conditions for being a proper Logic System (Definition
2
). By condition
2
.
3
,
{\kappa(\lnot\mathtt{R})}={\kappa(\mathtt{R})}^{c}
. Thus,

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}\cap({\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\cup{\kappa(\lnot\mathtt{R})})

(72)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}\cap{\kappa(\mathtt{R})})\cup{\kappa((\mathtt{Q})}\cap{\kappa(\lnot\mathtt{R})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})}\cup{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})}

(73)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))},

(74)

where (
72
) follows by the DeMorgan Laws for sets, (
73
) follows by condition
2
.
2
, and (
74
) follows by condition
2
.
1
. Then, to go from (c) to (d),
note that

\displaystyle{\kappa(\lnot((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))}^{c}

(75)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})}\cup{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})})^{c}

(76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})}^{c}\cap{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})}^{c}

(77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\kappa(\lnot(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R}))}\cap{\kappa(\lnot(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))},

(78)

where equality (
75
) follows from condition
2
.
3
, (
76
) follows by condition
2
.
1
, (
77
) follows by DeMorgan, and (
78
) follows by condition
2
.
3
. Putting these together, it follows that
\vec{\kappa}((\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))_{i}=0
iff
[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]
. Here we use the fact that if
\mathtt{s}\in\mathcal{L}
is a sentence, then
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s})_{i}=0
iff
\vec{\kappa}(\lnot\mathtt{s})_{i}=1
, which follows from condition
2
.
1
,
{\kappa(\lnot\mathtt{s})}={\kappa(\mathtt{s})}^{c}
, since taking the complement of the kernel corresponds to flipping 1s to 0s and 0s to 1s in the vector notation.

To obtain (e), note that

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]

\displaystyle\left[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0\right]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]

From this observation one then may trivially write

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]

\displaystyle\left[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0\right]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0]

from which the assertion follows. Finally (f) follows from the expected average kernel sizes as given in the theorem, in conjunction with the “identically distributed” assumption. Specifically,

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=0|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1])P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1)}{P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1])}{P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r}

with a parallel derivation applicable for the other equation in (f).

Similarly, one can write

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}((\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})\lor(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R}))_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]\cup[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})+P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=1|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=1)&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1\\
P([\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})_{i}=0)&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p_{s^{*}}/p_{r}&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1\\
p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r})&\mbox{ if }\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0\end{array}\right.

Steps (a-d) can be justified using arguments very similar to those of the previous derivation. Step (e) follows from the observation that

\displaystyle[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]\cap[\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}\land\lnot\mathtt{R})_{i}=1]=\emptyset

Step (f) can be seen by noting that if
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1
, then the second term of (e) is zero, and if
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0
, then the first term of (e) is zero. Step (g) follows from the expected average kernel sizes and the identically distributed assumption, using arguments similar to those of in step (f) of the earlier derivation.

By construction, the
X_{i}
s are mutually independent conditional on
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})
. Using this fact, we can now write

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(g(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{r}),\mathtt{r}))|\mathtt{R}=\mathtt{r})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle I(X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}H(X_{i}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))-\sum_{i}H(X_{i}|Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}X_{1}^{i-1},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i:\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))+\sum_{i:\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})=\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|\min_{X\sim(1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r},p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},p_{q^{*}}/p_{r}-p_{s^{*}}/p_{r}),Q_{Z|X}:E[\rho(X,Z)]=0}I(X;Z)

\displaystyle+|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})^{c}|\min_{X\sim(1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r})-p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r})),Q_{Z|X}:E[\rho(X,Z)]=0}I(X;Z)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})+|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})^{c}|\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r})).

Finally note that
E_{\mathtt{R}}\left[|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\right]=|\mathcal{M}|p_{r}

and thus putting everything together

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|p_{r}\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})+|\mathcal{M}|(1-p_{r})\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}},p_{r}-p_{q^{*}})+|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s^{**}},1-p_{r}-p_{q^{**}}).

We now prove the upper bound, which we emphasize, will be developed under the weaker assumption that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
follow a
(p_{r},p_{s^{*}},p_{s^{**}},p_{q^{*}},p_{q^{**}})
-law. Let
f
and
g
be the encoder and decoder functions that we will be constructing. Let
\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r}\in\mathcal{L}
and define

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}\mathbf{\land}\mathtt{r}

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}^{**}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{s}\mathbf{\land}\mathbf{\lnot}\mathtt{r}

\displaystyle\mathtt{q}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{q}\mathbf{\land}\mathtt{r}

\displaystyle\mathtt{q}^{**}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathtt{q}\mathbf{\land}\mathbf{\lnot}\mathtt{r}.

We similarly define random versions of the above by replacing the lower case letters with upper case letters. We can compute, for the random versions, the corresponding normalized expected kernel sizes:

\displaystyle p_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}|

\displaystyle p_{s^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{S}^{*})}|

\displaystyle p_{s^{**}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{S}^{**})}|

\displaystyle p_{q^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}^{*})}|

\displaystyle p_{q^{**}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E|{\kappa(\mathtt{Q}^{**})}|.

Also define

\displaystyle j_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{i:\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=0\}

\displaystyle j_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{i:\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})_{i}=1\}

\displaystyle x^{*}_{1}\cdots x^{*}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s}^{*}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q}^{*}))\right]_{j_{1}}

\displaystyle x^{**}_{1}\cdots x^{**}_{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s}^{**}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q}^{**}))]_{j_{0}}

and as before, define random versions by replacing lower case letters with upper case letters.
Using Theorem

4
twice we can guarantee the existence of
f^{*}
,
g^{*}
,
f^{**}
,
g^{**}
such that

\displaystyle E\left[{\mathbf{len}(f^{*}(X^{*}_{1}\cdots X^{*}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}))}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})+

\displaystyle+2\log_{2}\left(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})\right)+3

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}\rho(X_{i}^{*},g^{*}(f^{*}(X_{1}^{*}\cdots X_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}^{*}))_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle E\left[{\mathbf{len}(f^{**}(X^{**}_{1}\cdots X^{**}_{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}))}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))

\displaystyle+2\log_{2}\left((|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))\right)+3

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}\rho(X_{i}^{**},g^{**}(f^{**}(X_{1}^{**}\cdots X_{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}^{**}))_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Next we assemble an encoder and decoder for our setup. Given two binary strings
c_{1},c_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, let
c_{1}c_{2}
denote the string resulting from concatenating the two individual strings. We define our encoder as

\displaystyle f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r})=f^{*}(x^{*}_{1}\cdots x^{**}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|})f^{**}(x^{**}_{1}\cdots x^{**}_{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|}).

From our earlier estimates,

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})

\displaystyle+(|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))+6

\displaystyle+2\log_{2}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})

\displaystyle+2\log_{2}\left((|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))\right)

and using the law of total expectations, and the concavity
\cap
of the logarithm, we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}))}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p_{r}\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})

\displaystyle+(1-p_{r})\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))+6|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}

\displaystyle+2|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\log_{2}\left(p_{r}|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s^{*}}/p_{r},1-p_{q^{*}}/p_{r})\right)

\displaystyle+2|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\log_{2}\left((1-p_{r})|\mathcal{M}|\Lambda(p_{s^{**}}/(1-p_{r}),1-p_{q^{**}}/(1-p_{r}))\right).

Now we construct the decoder. We define a function
lift
, on two arguments: a
filter
and a
pattern
. The function lifts a binary
pattern
to into a larger binary vector using the positions indicated by
filter
, maintaining the order of the bits in the
pattern
. For a vector
\textit{filter}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
and
\textit{pattern}\in\{0,1\}^{|\textit{filter}|}
(where
|\textit{filter}|
indicates the number of ones in the vector
filter
), let
a_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,|\textit{filter}|
, be the positions such that
\textit{filter}_{a_{i}}=1
. We then define

\displaystyle\textit{lift}(\textit{filter},\textit{pattern})_{a_{i}}=\textit{pattern}_{i}

and define
lift
to be
0
in all other indices.

The code that Theorem
4
guarantees existence of is prefix free, and thus the output of
f(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})
can be decoded sequentially. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{lift}\left(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}),g^{*}\left(f^{*}\left(x^{*}_{1}\cdots x^{*}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{lift}\left(1-\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}),g^{**}\left(f^{**}\left(x^{**}_{1}\cdots x^{**}_{|\mathcal{M}|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})|}\right)\right)\right).

Now, assume that
\mathtt{q}
is such that
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{q}
. Then by we know that

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s})\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r})\leq\mathbf{a}\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q})\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}),

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s})(1-\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))\leq\mathbf{b}\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q})(1-\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{r}))

where the products and inequalities are interpreted element-wise. And as a consequence

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{s})\leq\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{q}).

The output of the decoder
g
is defined to be

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}).

This ensures that the requirement
g(f(\mathtt{s},\mathtt{q},\mathtt{r}))\vdash\mathtt{q}
is satisfied.
\hfill\square

5.7
General setup when Alice does not know what Bob knows

To conclude our sequence of theorems, we study a situation where Alice does not know the specific sentence
\mathtt{R}
knows, and is trying to ensure that Bob knows how to prove a targeted query
\mathtt{Q}
. This setup can be seen as a combination of the setups in Theorem
5
and Theorem
6
, and it is the most complex situation that we analyze from the standpoint of the mathematical machinery used to prove it.

In a manner similar to the discussion leading to Theorem
6
, we rely on Table
2
in order to define the communication pattern that we analyze. A total of 6 turns are allowed, starting with Bob making the first communication and concluding with Bob being able to prove a targeted query
\mathtt{Q}
. The outputs for all the encoders are denoted by
B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3},B^{4},A^{5}
. The cost of communication is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{4})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{5})}\right].

The image of all of the encoders is assumed to be prefix-free, assuming that whatever is the common context is kept fixed when considering the image. For a more detailed discussion of this, note the comments associated with Table
1
. A 6-turn code
\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{5}
as defined by Table
2
and this discussion is called a 6-turn code for targeted queries. The reason for the word “targeted” is because the goal for the communication can be restricted to be a subset of Alice’s knowledge, rather than its entirety.

Theorem 8

Let
(L,\mathcal{M},\kappa,\ell)
be a Logic System. Let
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}\in\mathcal{L}
represent the sender, query and receiver logic sentences, with
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q}
only known to Alice and
\mathtt{R}
only known to Bob. Assume that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
have kernels that follow a (
p_{s},p_{q},p_{r}
)-law. Then

\displaystyle\min_{\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{5}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{4})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{5})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right),

where the minimization is for all 6-turn codes for targeted queries (c.f. Table
2
). Furthermore, if
\{(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
are i.i.d. and
\mathtt{R}\rightarrow{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}\rightarrow({\kappa(\mathtt{S})},{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})})
then

\displaystyle\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\min_{\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{5}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{4})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{5})}\right]

Proof.
We start the proof using the beginning arguments from the proof of the lower bound of Theorem
6
. We assume a 6-turn code for targeted queries
\{f^{i},g^{i}\}_{i=0}^{5}
, and let
B^{0},A^{1},B^{2},A^{3},B^{4},A^{5}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
be the binary strings output by the encoders
f^{0},f^{1},f^{2},f^{3},f^{4},f^{5}
that are employed by Bob and Alice. Using the same type of argument that led to (
55
), we have

\displaystyle E_{\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}}\left[{\mathbf{len}(B^{0})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{1})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{2})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{3})}+{\mathbf{len}(B^{4})}+{\mathbf{len}(A^{5})}\right]\geq H(\mathtt{\hat{S}}|\mathtt{R}).

We now continue by using a more complex variant of the arguments found in the proof of Theorem
5
. We write

\displaystyle H(\mathtt{\hat{S}}|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\mathtt{R})-H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle I(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q};\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\mathtt{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\mathtt{R})-H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\mathtt{R},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))-H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\mathtt{R},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))-H(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(h)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))

where (a) follows from the fact that deterministic functions of random quantities cannot increase entropy, (b) holds because in the second term, the conditioning is over all randomness, rendering the corresponding entropy equal to zero, (c) is the definition of mutual information, (d) follows from the data processing inequality, (e) expands mutual information back into a difference of entropies, (f) follows from the assumption that

\displaystyle\mathtt{R}\rightarrow\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\rightarrow(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}))

as well as the data processing inequality, (g) follows from the fact that a weaker conditioning cannot reduce conditional entropy, and (h) is from the definition of mutual information.

In reference to the proof of Theorem
5
, recall that for a given
\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{L}
,
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{a})
can be regarded as an indicator vector for the kernel
{\kappa(\mathtt{a})}
. Additionally, we use the same definition for
\psi(\cdot,\cdot)
:

\displaystyle\psi(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})=0\times(\underline{1}-\mathtt{b})+1\times\mathtt{a}+2\times(\underline{1}-\mathtt{a})\times\mathtt{b}.

where
(\underline{1}-\mathtt{a})\times\mathtt{b}
is interpreted to be product element-wise. We now define

\displaystyle X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}))

\displaystyle Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}).

{\kappa(\mathtt{Q})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{S})}
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}

Figure 20
:
Reference diagram for the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
8

To interpret the first definition we refer the reader to Figure
20
, which is a counterpart to Figure
16
-b where we have added
{\kappa(\mathtt{R})}
. As in that figure, the red region is assigned “0”, the green region “1” and the white region “2”.

The next sequence of arguments is a simplified version of those in Theorem
7
. For any kernel
k\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
:

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=0|Y_{i}=k_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=0|Y_{i}=k_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=1|Y_{i}=k_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{P(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=1)P(Y_{i}=k_{i}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i}=1)}{P(Y_{i}=k_{i})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mbox{ if }k_{i}=0\\
1-p_{q}/p_{r}&\mbox{ if }k_{i}=1\end{array}\right.

where (a) follows from the Definition
X_{i}=\psi(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})_{i},\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})_{i})
and the independence assumption, (b) follows from Lemma
6
, and (c) follows from the identically distributed assumption and the
(p_{s},p_{q},p_{r})
-law. Similarly, one can deduce

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=1|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)=P(X_{i}=1|Y_{i}=1)=p_{s}/p_{r}

(84)

and therefore

\displaystyle P(X_{i}=2|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)=P(X_{i}=2|Y_{i}=1)=p_{q}/p_{r}-p_{s}/p_{r}

(85)

One consequence of this analysis is that, conditional on
Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k
, the
\{X_{i}\}
are independent although not generally identically distributed. Recall we are assuming that whenever using the 6-turn code for targeted queries,
\mathtt{S}\vdash\mathtt{\hat{S}}
and that
\mathtt{\hat{S}}\vdash\mathtt{Q}
and recall the definition of the distortion metric
\rho
from Equations (
46
) and (
47
). Then for all
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{M}|
,

\displaystyle P(Z_{i}=0|X_{i}=0,Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle P(Z_{i}=1|X_{i}=1,Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

and therefore as a consequence, for all
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{M}|
, under the conditioning
Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k
, with probability 1,

\displaystyle\rho(X_{i},Z_{i})=0.

(86)

Following in the footsteps of the proof of the lower bound for Theorem
5
, we write:

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{\hat{S}})|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q});Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle I(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k}P(Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)I(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k).

The step (i) follows from the fact that
X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
is a deterministic function of
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})
and
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})
, and thus conditional on
Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
, the following Markov chain holds:

\displaystyle X_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\rightarrow(\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}))\rightarrow Z_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}.

Step (j) follows from the definition of conditional mutual information. We then focus on each individual term:

\displaystyle I(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|};Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)-H(X_{1},\ldots,X_{|\mathcal{M}|}|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|},Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(k)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}H(X_{i}|X_{1}^{i-1},Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)-H(X_{i}|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|},X_{1}^{i-1},Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(l)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}H(X_{i}|Y_{i}=k_{i})-H(X_{i}|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{|\mathcal{M}|},X_{1}^{i-1},Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(m)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}H(X_{i}|Y_{i}=k_{i})-H(X_{i}|Z_{i},Y_{i}=k_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=k_{i})

where (k) follows from the chain rule for entropy, (l) follows from the fact that the
|\mathcal{M}|
tuples
\{(X,Y)_{i}\}
are statistically independent as per the Theorem’s assumption. Finally (m) follows from the fact that eliminating conditioning random variables cannot decrease entropy. We continue by splitting the last summation in two:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=k_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i:k_{i}=0}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=0)+\sum_{i:k_{i}=1}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=1)

(87)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i:k_{i}=1}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=1)

where we have made the observation that conditioning on
Y_{i}=0
,
X_{i}
is constant (and equal to zero) and therefore the corresponding mutual information is zero.

As before, to complete the lower bound, we note that (
87
) is an averaging of mutual informations where the marginal for
X_{i}
is identical for all
\{i:k_{i}=1\}
, as given by Equations (
5.7
,
84
,
85
), but the conditionals
Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i},Y_{i}=1}
are in general different. Assume temporarily that
|k|=|\{i:k_{i}=1\}|\geq 1
. Define a conditional distribution by averaging all those conditionals:

\displaystyle Q_{Z^{\prime}|X^{\prime}}(z|x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{|k|}\sum_{\{i:k_{i}=1\}}Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i},Y_{i}}(z|x,y=1).

(88)

In reference to Equations (
5.7
,
84
,
85
), let
X^{\prime}
be distributed according to
\{1-p_{q}/p_{r},p_{s}/p_{r},p_{q}/p_{r}-p_{s}/p_{r}\}
and let the joint
X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
be defined by having
Z^{\prime}
be the effect of passing
X^{\prime}
through the channel defined by (
88
). Because of (
86
), it is the case that

\displaystyle E_{X^{\prime},Z^{\prime}}\left[\rho(X^{\prime},Z^{\prime})\right]=0.

Temporarily imagine
I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=1)
as a function of two distributions: a distribution on
X_{i}
given
Y_{i}=1
and a conditional distribution
Q_{Z_{i}|X_{i},Y_{i}}(z|x,y=1)
. Given our earlier arguments, in (
87
), the distribution of
X_{i}
given
Y_{i}=1
is identical for all those
\{i:k_{i}=1\}
, and precisely equal to that of
X^{\prime}
. Furthermore, it is known that mutual information is convex
\cup
if one keeps the marginal distribution of
X
fixed as one varies the conditional distribution of
Z
given
X
and therefore the following bound holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{\{i:k_{i}=1\}}I(X_{i};Z_{i}|Y_{i}=1)\geq|k|I(X^{\prime};Z^{\prime})\geq|k|\min_{P(X,Z)\in\mathcal{D}}I(X;Z)

(89)

where the domain
\mathcal{D}
for the minimization is defined by joint distributions for
X,Z
with
X\sim(1-p_{q}/p_{r},p_{s}/p_{r},p_{q}/p_{r}-p_{s}/p_{r})
and
E[\rho(X,Z)]=0
. Also note that even though we assumed
|k|\geq 1
, the bound (
89
) is trivially true for
|k|=0
due to the nonnegativity of mutual information. Such minimization, which can be obtained using standard variational methods, results in the following expression

\displaystyle|k|\Lambda(p_{s}/p_{r},1-p_{q}/p_{r}).

We now bring back the distribution for
k
, and write

\displaystyle\sum_{k}P(Y_{1}^{|\mathcal{M}|}=k)|k|\Lambda(p_{s}/p_{r},1-p_{q}/p_{r})=p_{r}\Lambda(p_{s}/p_{r},1-p_{q}/p_{r})=\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})

where the last equality follows from basic properties of
\Lambda
(Lemma
2
). This concludes the proof of the lower bound.

The proof of the upper bound is adapted from the proof of Theorem
6
. We stress that this result will hold under the very general assumption that
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}
follow a
(p_{s},p_{q},p_{r})
-law with
p_{s}\leq p_{q}\leq p_{r}
. As before, we establish the precise domain/image of the encoder and decoders for our 6-turn mode:

\displaystyle f^{0}:\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{0}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle f^{1}:\mathcal{L}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{1}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2},

\displaystyle f^{2}:\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{2}:\{0,1\}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle f^{3}:\mathcal{L}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{3}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{3}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\times\{\text{success},\text{failure}\},

\displaystyle f^{4}:\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{4}:\{0,1\}\rightarrow\{\text{success},\text{failure}\},

\displaystyle f^{5}:\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\{0,1\}^{*},\hskip 7.22743ptg^{5}:\{0,1\}^{*}\times\{\text{success},\text{failure}\}\times\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}.

A bin of size 1
(out of 32)
A bin of size 2
(out of 16)
A bin of size 4
(out of 8)

Figure 21
:
An adaptive rate hash function. An initial hash function, depicted at the bottom, can be used to create a family of hash functions by stacking halves iteratively. A total of three hash functions are shown; an exemplary bin for each is illustrated using a dashed box.

In reference to the proof of Theorem
6
, a key step is the definition of a hash function. For the present result we will require an
adaptive rate
hash function which allows the number of bins to be flexibly changed in response to specific requirements. We refer the reader to Figure
21
for this construction. A hash function with
2^{i}
bins for some integer
i
, requiring
i
bits to specify a specific bin, can also behave as a hash function with
2^{i-1}
bins by stacking the second half of the bins for the former on top of the left half, creating half the bins with twice the number of elements and thus requiring one bit less to fully specify a bin. This stacking procedure can be continued, creating a family of hash functions with fewer bit requirements from a single hash function. Given any desired bit rate, provided that it is equal or lower than the maximum bit rate that the hash function supports, the adaptive hash function will be able match that desired bit rate within at most 1 bit of inefficiency. Define

\displaystyle T(w)=\{c\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{M}|}:~|c|=w\}.

Alice and Bob first interact to agree on the following functions for integers
0<s\leq q\leq r

\displaystyle\text{weight}(s,q,r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lceil\frac{rs}{s+r-q}\right\rceil

\displaystyle\text{code\_size}(s,q,r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lceil\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\mathcal{M}|\\
\text{weight}(s,q,r)\end{array}\right)/\left(\begin{array}[]{c}q-s\\
\text{weight}(s,q,r)-s\end{array}\right)\right\rceil

as well as

•

a set of codewords
\text{code}(s,q,r)\subseteq T(\text{weight}(s,q,r))
, indexed using integers
1,\ldots,\text{code\_size}(s,q,r)

•

an adaptive rate hash function
\text{bin}_{s,q,r,\text{rate}}:\text{code}(s,q,r)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.

From this definition it should be clear that a codeword is an element of
\{0,1\}^{|M|}
. In our paper, a codeword is equivalent to a kernel, since it can be seen as the indicator vector of the kernel. Thus a code can be seen as a set of kernels. How these are constructed will be described shortly. For two vectors
a,b
of the same length, we will write
a\leq b
whenever
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for every
i
. The protocol is then as follows:

1.

The receiver sends
|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|
to the sender, which she decodes. This step defines
f^{0}
and
g^{0}
.

2.

The sender sends
|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|
and
|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|
, which are decoded at the receiver side. This defines
f^{1}
and
g^{1}
.

3.

Both sender and receiver compute a target weight and a code book size using the functions (
5.7
) evaluated on
|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|
, and
|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|
.

4.

The receiver calculates the average bit rate it wants from the receiver using the expression

\displaystyle\text{RATE}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\log_{2}\left\lceil|\left\{a\in\text{code}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|):a\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right\}|\right\rceil+\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

(95)

Note the “round up” operator inside of the logarithm. This implies, in particular, that
2^{|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}
will be a proper power of 2, and hence the resulting rate can be used in the context of the adaptive rate hash function. The integer
|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}
is sent back to the sender, defining
f^{2}
and
g^{2}
. At this point they both agree on target weight, code book size and target rate to be used in the adaptive rate hash function.

5.

The sender attempts to find
A\in\text{code}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)
with the property that

\displaystyle\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})\leq A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}).

(96)

If this fails, then the sender signals the failure to the receiver and executes step
9
. If it succeeds, the sender signals success and sends to the receiver the bin to which
A
was mapped, for the hash function with the agreed upon rate.

6.

The receiver attempts to retrieve
A
using the bin index, its knowledge of
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})
and the algorithm described below. The outcome of this attempt results in an element of
\mathcal{L}
and a “success” or a default, dummy element from
\mathcal{L}
and a “failure”. Together with the previous step, this completes
f^{3},g^{3}
.

7.

The success/failure of the attempt is signaled back to the sender using a single bit. This defines both
f^{4}
and
g^{4}
.

8.

If successful, the sender has nothing to do anymore, as the receiver expects no further communication. The receiver simply outputs the element from
\mathcal{L}
computed by
g^{3}
, designating it as the output
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
, partly defining
g^{5}
.

9.

If unsuccessful, the sender sends
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})
as a binary vector of length
|\mathcal{M}|
, which is decoded by the receiver and becomes
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
, the designated output of the protocol, completing the definition of
f^{5}
and
g^{5}
.

The algorithm guarantees that the receiver at the end will have in possession enough information to reproduce a message
\mathtt{\hat{S}}
that can be used to prove
\mathtt{Q}
. We now demonstrate that the normalized total bidirectional cost in bits is asymptotically
\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})
.

Instead of constructing a specific
\text{code}(s,q,r)
and hash function
\text{bin}_{s,q,r,\text{rate}}
we will define a probability distribution over each of these, evaluate the corresponding expected performance, and demonstrate that in average the performance as desired, implying the existence of deterministic versions of these with at least the same performance. We shall refer to the random constructions as
\text{CODE}(s,q,r)
and
\text{BIN}_{s,q,r,\text{rate}}
, respectively.

We construct
\text{CODE}(s,q,r)
by drawing uniformly and independently at random from
T(\text{weight}(s,q,r))
a total of
\text{code\_size}(s,q,r)
codewords. We construct
\text{BIN}_{s,q,r,\text{rate}}
by mapping every element of
\text{CODE}(s,q,r)
uniformly and independently at random to an integer in the range
\{1,\ldots,2^{\lceil\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|\rceil}\}
(each representing a bin), and then iteratively stacking halves of these bins to produce an adaptive rate hash function, as illustrated by Figure
21
.

For brevity, define

\displaystyle W=\text{weight}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|).

In the algorithm above, there are two key failure events for which we want to estimate probabilities. First we focus on the probability of a failure in step
5
.
The probability that any one element of
\mathcal{C}
satisfies (
96
) is

\displaystyle P_{succ}=\frac{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|\\
W-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|\end{array}\right)}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\mathcal{M}|\\
W\end{array}\right)}.

The probability that none of the elements of
\mathcal{C}
meet the condition (
96
) is then

\displaystyle(1-P_{succ})^{|\mathcal{C}|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-P_{succ}|\mathcal{C}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}

where we used the definition (
5.7
) to obtain the latter. Now we focus on the failure probability in step
6
. Define

\displaystyle\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\left\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}:\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R}),\text{BIN}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}(\alpha)=\text{BIN}_{|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|}(A)\right\}.

(98)

To estimate the failure probability, we upper bound the probability of this event:

\displaystyle P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right).

Let
I_{\alpha}
be equal to 1 if
\alpha\in\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})
and 0 otherwise. Then we can upper bound the error probability as

\displaystyle P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{\alpha}I_{\alpha}\geq 2\right).

We note that when conditioning on
\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},A,\mathcal{C}
, the only randomness that remains in (
98
) is that of the randomness of bin assignments. We then write

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{\alpha}I_{\alpha}\geq 2\lvert\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},A,\mathcal{C}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-P\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\neq A,\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})}I_{\alpha}=0\lvert\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},A,\mathcal{C}\right)

(99)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\neq A,\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})}P(I_{\alpha}=0|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},A,\mathcal{C})

(100)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\neq A,\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})}\left(1-2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1-\Pi_{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})}\left(1-2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\left(1-2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}\right)^{|\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\}|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}}|\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-|\mathcal{M}|\text{RATE}+\log_{2}|\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C},\alpha\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

(101)

As in the proof of Theorem
6
, the derivation above is the essence of the theorem. The most delicate step is the one that leads from (
99
) to (
100
), where independence of the
\{I_{\alpha}\}\setminus\{A\}
events under the given conditioning is invoked to rewrite the probability of the event as a product of probabilities. The core reason why this independence holds is because under the given conditioning, the bins to which any one
\alpha
is mapped are chosen independently from each other over the set of possible bins. Since these steps are very similar to those in Theorem
3
, we skip most explanations and simply point out that the final expression (
101
) comes from the definition (
95
). In summary, we now have that

\displaystyle P\left(\left[|\text{hypotheses}(\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R})|\geq 2\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}.

We can now account for all of the bits sent in the protocol. The biggest contribution to the bit rate is that of step
5
:

\displaystyle E\left[\text{RATE}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\log_{2}|\left\{A\in\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|):A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right\}|\right]+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}+\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left[\left.E\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\log_{2}|\left\{A\in\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|):A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right\}|\right|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}\right]\right]+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\left.\log_{2}E\left[|\left\{A\in\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|):A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right\}|\right|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}\right]\right]+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right).

We recall that the way
\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)
is constructed is by choosing uniformly at random

\displaystyle\text{code\_size}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)

(102)

elements from
T(W)
. Next note that

\displaystyle|\{A:A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\}\cap T(W)|=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\\
W\end{array}\right).

Any one element of
T(W)
has a probability of

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\mathcal{M}|\\
W\end{array}\right)^{-1}

The average number of elements of
\{A:A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\}
chosen to be part of
\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|)
, conditional on
\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q}),\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})
is then given by the product of (
102
), (
5.7
) and (
5.7
):

\displaystyle\log_{2}E\left[\left.|\left\{A\in\text{CODE}(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|):A\leq\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})\right\}|\right|\mathtt{S},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left(\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|\\
W\end{array}\right)/\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|\\
W-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|\end{array}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|+|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|)H_{\text{bin}}\left(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|/(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|+|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|)\right)+\log_{2}\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda(|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{S})|,|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{R})|-|\vec{\kappa}(\mathtt{Q})|)+\log_{2}\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|.

Further taking the expectation over the remaining randomness, using the concavity
\cap
of the
\Lambda
function (see Lemma
2
), and normalizing, we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle E\left[\text{RATE}\right]\leq\Lambda(p_{s},p_{r}-p_{q})+O\left(\frac{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|}{|\mathcal{M}|}\right).

The cost of steps
1
,
2
,
4
can be verified to be
O(|\mathcal{M}|^{-1}\log|\mathcal{M}|)
by using

\delta
Elias coding. Finally, in the case of failure when trying to find an
A
meeting (
96
) the cost is
O(|\mathcal{M}|^{-1})
given (
5.7
).
\hfill\square

6
Formal logical underpinnings

In this section we formally define what we mean by a logic, something that we only introduced informally in the description preceding Definition
1
of a Logic System. To make the definition of a logic rigorous, we introduce several additional concepts, including: what a structure is (Definition
9
), what it means for a structure to model a set of logic sentences (Definition
15
), what it means for a logic sentence to be true (Definition
16
), the notions of a logic being sound and complete (Definitions
21
and
23
), and several subtle variations on these latter two notions (Definitions
22
,
24
, and
25
). Two key results of this section are Theorem
9
, which shows that condition (
6
) of Definition
1
is equivalent to strong soundness and a type of strong completeness of the underlying logic, and Lemma
7
, which gives general conditions under which a Logic System can be guaranteed to satisfy conditions
1
-
3
of Definition
2
, and hence be deemed a
proper
Logic System.

With the aim of developing as all-encompassing a notion of what constitutes a logic as possible, we will establish a set of definitions that set us up well for First-Order Logic and logics that “extend” First-Order Logic, such as second and higher-order logics
[
73
]
2
2

2

Logics that we will, however, not consider formally, other than to say that they extend First-Order Logic in the sense of Definition
11
.
. Towards the end of our development (Subsection
6.3
), we will see that one can fit Propositional Logic and other quantifier-free logics into this model as well. Among other things, at the end of this section we will be able to conclude that all the results of this paper apply to Propositional Logic on a fixed number of variables as well as the First-Order Logic of structures of fixed finite sizes.

6.1
The notion of a Logic

By a
logic
,
L=(\tau,\mathbb{S},\mathcal{T},\sigma,\mathcal{P})
, we mean (i) a vocabulary, or set of symbols,
\tau
, (ii) a syntax,
\mathbb{S}
, or set of rules for combining the elements of
\tau
together to form “well-formed sentences”, (iii) a definition of truth,
\mathcal{T}
, or set of rules describing how truth propagates through logical symbols, such as
\land,\lor,\lnot,\forall
and
\exists
, that are part of the vocabulary, (iv) a set of axioms,
\sigma
(with, possibly,
\sigma=\varnothing
), or sentences that are assumed to be true without proof, and finally (v) a proof system,
\mathcal{P}
, comprising a set of rules of inference, for establishing when certain well-formed sentences follow from other well-formed sentences.
As in the definition of a Logic System, we refer to the set of well-formed sentences over
\tau
, given the syntax of
L
, by
\mathcal{L}
. We denote the entailment operator associated with the proof system
\mathcal{P}
by
\vdash
(or by
\vdash_{\mathcal{P}}
if the associated proof system is not entirely clear). The meta-mathematical expression
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{t}
means that the sentence
\mathtt{t}
can be proved from
\sigma\cup\{\mathtt{s}\}
using
\mathcal{P}
. The symbol
\vdash
is not part of any logical vocabulary and hence the expression
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{t}
is not a sentence of any logical language – hence our assertion that the expression
\mathtt{s}\vdash\mathtt{t}
is a “meta-mathematical expression”. As noted in Subsection
2.1
, we can apply the entailment operator,
\vdash
, to sets of sentences as well as to individual sentences.

We described the vocabulary and syntax of classical Propositional Logic in Subsection
2.1
.
A second important logic that we will consider is First-Order Logic. Although the vocabulary of First-Order Logic includes the logical connectives and grouping parentheses of Propositional Logic, the vocabularies of these two logics are otherwise quite distinct. The vocabulary of First-Order Logic does
not
include propositional variables, but adds the universal and existential quantifiers,
\forall
and
\exists
, as well as a countable number of variables,
x_{i}
, that can be associated with the quantifiers. The vocabulary of First-Order Logic also includes function and relation symbols of different arities,
f^{a_{i}}_{i}
and
R^{\alpha_{i}}_{i}
, as well as constant symbols,
c_{i}
. As is customary, we will always assume that there is a distinguished binary equality relation symbol
{=}(\cdot,\cdot)
, and per convention, write
x=y
for
{=}(x,y)
and
x\neq y
for
{\lnot}{=}(x,y)
. A vocabulary that includes the logical connectives of Propositional Logic, parentheses, a countable number of variable symbols, the quantifiers
\forall
and
\exists
, as well as a particular set of function, relation and constant symbols (the relations symbols necessarily including
{=}(\cdot,\cdot)
), is called a
First-Order vocabulary
. When it is understood that a vocabulary,
\tau
, is First-Order, one typically writes
\tau=(\{f_{i}^{a_{i}}\},\{R_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\},\{c_{i}\})
, specifying just the non-logical symbols that distinguish
\tau
from other First-Order vocabularies. One of the simplest First-Order vocabularies is the vocabulary of directed graphs. In addition to the built-in equality symbol, this vocabulary consists of a single binary relation symbol,
E(\cdot,\cdot)
. In this case we would write
\tau=(\varnothing,\{E(\cdot,\cdot)\},\varnothing)
, or, equivalently,
\tau=(\varnothing,\{E^{2}\},\varnothing)
. In some cases when we work with graphs, we may be considering, say, shortest path, or connectivity questions between specified nodes. In this case it may be convenient to expand the vocabulary to include constants, which are typically denoted
s
and
t
(for “source” and “target”). The vocabulary would then be denoted
\tau=(\varnothing,\{E(\cdot,\cdot)\},\{s,t\})
.

The concept of a “variable” in First-Order Logic is completely different from the concept of a variable in Propositional Logic, as is illustrated by their vastly different syntax, or rules for sentence formation. To understand the syntax of First-Order Logic, we build up a set of definitions.

Definition 4

A
term
is defined inductively as follows:

•

Each variable or constant symbol is a term;

•

If
f^{m}
is an
m
-ary function symbol, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
are terms, then
f^{m}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
is also a term.

Definition 5

A
primitive formula
3
3

3

Sometimes referred to as an atomic formula.
in First-Order Logic is a string of symbols of the form
R^{m}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
where
R^{m}
is an
m
-ary relation symbol (possibly the binary equality symbol
{=}(t_{1},t_{2})
) and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
are terms. Analogously, we refer to a primitive formula that does not contain variables as a
primitive sentence
.

Definition 6

A string of symbols is a
formula
of First-Order Logic if it is either a primitive formula or can be constructed from primitive formulas by repeated application of the following rules:

R1.

If
\phi
is a formula, then so is
\lnot\phi
;

R2.

If
\phi
and
\psi
are formulas, then so are
\phi\lor\psi
and
\phi\land\psi
;

R3.

If
\phi
is a formula, then so are
\exists x\phi
and
\forall x\phi
.

The same symbol in a First-Order vocabulary can appear in multiple positions within a given formula. We refer to each appearance of the same symbol as an
occurrence
of the symbol.

Definition 7

We recursively define the notion of an occurrence of a variable being either
free
(equivalently,
unbound
) or
bound
to a quantifier within a formula. If an occurrence of
x
in a formula
\phi
is free, the variable
x
is also said to
occur freely
in
\phi
. In a formula
\phi
